Smoothness spaces for warped time-frequency representations—Decomposition spaces and embedding relations Thanks: This work was supported by the Austrian Science Fund (FWF): I 3067–N30. FV acknowledges support by the German Science Foundation (DFG) in the context of the Emmy Noether junior research group VO 2594/1–1. NH is grateful for the hospitality and support of the Katholische Universität Eichstätt-Ingolstadt during his visit. FV would like to thank the Acoustics Research Institute for the hospitality during several visits, which were partially supported by the Austrian Science Fund (FWF): 31225–N32.
Nicki Holighaus † ‡, Felix Voigtlaender ∗ ‡

Abstract
. In a recent paper, we have shown that warped time-frequency representations provide a rich framework for the construction and study of smoothness spaces matched to very general phase space geometries obtained by diffeomorphic deformations of ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} . Here, we study these spaces, obtained through the application of general coorbit theory, using the framework of decomposition spaces. This allows us to derive embedding relations between coorbit spaces associated to different warping functions, and relate them to established, important smothness spaces. In particular, we show that we obtain α \alpha -modulation spaces and spaces of dominating mixed smoothness as special cases and, in contrast, that this is only possible for Besov spaces if d = 1 d=1 .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
42B35, 46E35, 46F05, 46F12

3
3
footnotetext:
Both authors contributed equally to this work.

1.
Introduction

Warped time-frequency systems
were introduced in
[
21
,
20
,
19
]
as a means for the construction of time-frequency representations adapted to rather general
frequency scales
. In these works, it was demonstrated that such systems yield continuous, tight Parseval
frames that admit the construction of general coorbit spaces under mild conditions. As translation-invariant spaces inducing a certain frequency scale, it seems natural to suspect a link of these
warped coorbit spaces
to decomposition spaces
[
7
,
3
]
.
Whereas coorbit theory
[
8
,
9
,
16
]
, and its generalization to abstract continuous
frames
[
11
,
29
,
22
,
20
]
, classifes a function
f

according to the size of its voice transform
V_{\Psi}f
with respect to some frame
\Psi
, decomposition spaces
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{u}^{q})
directly classify
functions with respect to their frequency localization, as determined by a rather general covering
\mathcal{Q}
of the
frequency domain. The definition of these decomposition spaces generalizes classical Besov spaces, replacing the usual dyadic covering by a more general one: Given a suitable covering
\mathcal{Q}=\left(Q_{i}\right)_{i\in I}

and a suitable partition of unity
\left(\varphi_{i}\right)_{i\in I}

subordinate to
\mathcal{Q}
, the norm on the decomposition space

\mathcal{D}\left(\mathcal{Q},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{u}^{q}\right)

is given by

\left\|g\right\|_{\mathcal{D}\left(\mathcal{Q},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{u}^{q}\right)}=\left\|\left(u_{i}\cdot\left\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left(\varphi_{i}\cdot\smash{\widehat{g}}\right)\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{p}}\right)_{i\in I}\right\|_{\ell^{q}},

where
u=\left(u_{i}\right)_{i\in I}
is a suitable weight. Thus, the norm
is obtained by a weighted
\ell^{q}
-norm of the
\mathbf{L}^{p}
-norms
of the frequency-localized pieces
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left(\varphi_{i}\cdot\widehat{g}\right)

of
g
, where the precise frequency localization is determined by
the covering
\mathcal{Q}
.

Relations between coorbit spaces associated to translation-invariant
time-frequency representations and decomposition spaces occur naturally, as previously observed for
(
\alpha
-)modulation spaces, see
[
2
,
7
,
15
,
4
,
33
]
, and
wavelet coorbit spaces, see
[
13
,
12
]
. It is intuitively plausible that if the frame
\Psi
has the same (or very similar) frequency localization as
described by
\mathcal{Q}
, then the coorbit space
\mathrm{Co}(\Psi,\mathbf{L}_{\kappa}^{p})
should coincide with a suitable
decomposition space
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{\tilde{u}}^{p})
.
In fact, several recent works
[
3
,
26
,
25
,
38
,
39
]
have considered certain sequence norms of frame coefficients, e.g., with respect to a suitable generalized shift-invariant system, for the characterization of decomposition spaces, in varying generality.

A rather powerful embedding theory
[
35
,
36
]

for decomposition spaces was recently developed by one of the authors. Using this theory, once a given function space
X

is identified as a decomposition space
X=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{u}^{q})
,
the existence of embeddings between
X
and any other decomposition space

\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{P},\mathbf{L}^{p_{2}},\ell_{v}^{q_{2}})
can in many cases
be decided by comparing certain geometric and combinatorial properties of the coverings
\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{P}
.
Embeddings into classical Sobolev spaces have also been considered
[
37
]
.

In the present paper, we specifically consider the coorbit spaces associated to
warped time-frequency systems
, introduced in
[
21
,
20
,
19
]
.
Given a subset
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, a diffeomorphism
\Phi\colon D\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and a prototype function
\theta\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
,
the warped time-frequency system
\mathcal{G}(\theta,\Phi)=\{g_{y,\omega}\}_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\omega\in D}
is defined by

(1.1)

g_{y,\omega}:=\mathbf{T}_{y}\left(\mathcal{F}^{-1}g_{\omega}\right)\quad\text{with}\quad g_{\omega}=|\det{\mathrm{D}\Phi}(\omega)|^{1/2}\cdot(\mathbf{T}_{\Phi(\omega)}\theta)\circ\Phi,\quad\text{for all }(y,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times D.

By virtue of composition with the diffeomorphism
\Phi
, referred to as
warping function
, the family
\{g_{\omega}\}_{\omega\in D}
covers the frequency domain
D
uniformly

in warped coordinates
, thereby defining a frequency scale
\Phi^{-1}
to which
\mathcal{G}(\theta,\Phi)
is naturally adapted.

Based on results in
[
18
]
, the authors have provided in
[
19
]
straightforward conditions for
\mathcal{G}(\theta,\Phi)\subset\mathcal{T}
to generate Banach coorbit spaces

\operatorname{Co}(\Phi,Y)=\{f\in\mathcal{R}:\|(\langle f,g_{y,\omega}\rangle)_{(y,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times D}\|_{Y}<\infty\}

and to obtain atomic decompositions in
\operatorname{Co}(\Phi,Y)
by sampling
\mathcal{G}(\theta,\Phi)
. Here,
\mathcal{T}
is a suitable Banach space of test functions, the reservoir

\mathcal{R}
is the space of continuous, conjugate-linear functionals on
\mathcal{T}
, and
Y
is a rich, solid Banach space. In particular, the coorbit spaces
\operatorname{Co}(\Phi,\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa})
, with respect to the weighted mixed-norm spaces
\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{v}
,
p,q\geq 1
are well-defined Banach spaces under certain conditions, see also Section
2.1
. In the present work, we combine these results with the embedding theory for decomposition spaces
[
36
]
.

1.1.
Main results

Our central result identifies the coorbit spaces
\operatorname{Co}(\Phi,\mathbf{L}^{p.q}_{\kappa})
associated with warped time-frequency representations, hereafter also referred to by the conveniently shorter, if slightly ambiguous, term
warped coorbit spaces
, with certain decomposition spaces. Crucially, we require a suitable covering
\mathcal{Q}
, induced by
\Phi
. In fact, by simply pulling back the uniform covering

\left(\delta\cdot B_{r}(\ell)\right)_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
(for suitable
r>0
) of the “warped frequency space”
\mathbb{R}^{d}
using
\Phi^{-1}
, one obtains a the desired covering.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
\Phi:D\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a diffeomorphism.
For
\delta,r>0
, the family

\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}:=\big(\,Q_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}\,\big)_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}},\quad\text{with }Q_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}:=\Phi^{-1}(\delta\cdot B_{r}(k)).

is called the
(\delta,r)
-fine frequency covering induced by
\Phi
.

The
(\delta,r)
-fine frequency covering
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
induced by
\Phi
is studied more detail in Section
3
. With this family of coverings, we obtain the following identification result, proven in Section
4
.

Theorem 1
.

Let
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
open,
\Phi\colon D\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-diffeomorphism and a
(d+1)
-admissible warping function with control weight
v_{0}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow[1,\infty)
, and let the weight function
\kappa:D\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
be such that
\kappa\circ\Phi^{-1}
is
\tilde{\kappa}
-moderate, for some submultiplicative, radially increasing weight
\tilde{\kappa}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Finally, fix
\delta>0
and
r>\sqrt{d}/2
and consider the
(\delta,r)
-fine frequency covering
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
induced by
\Phi
.

If we set
u=(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
with
u_{k}=\kappa(\Phi^{-1}(\delta k))\cdot[\det(\mathrm{D}\Phi^{-1}(\delta k))]^{\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{2}}
, then

\operatorname{Co}(\Phi,\mathbf{L}_{\kappa}^{p,q})=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{u}^{q}).

To ensure that the warped coorbit space
\operatorname{Co}(\Phi,\mathbf{L}_{\kappa}^{p,q})
is well-defined, the assumption that
\Phi
is
(d+1)
-admissible (see Definition
2.2
for details) was required in
[
19
, e.g., Theorem 6.1]
, as was the
\tilde{\kappa}
-moderateness of
\kappa\circ\Phi^{-1}
(note that we restrict here to weights
\kappa
that are independent of the time/space variable). Hence, the only additional requirement on
\Phi
is that it is
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, which can always be achieved with arbitrarily small changes to
\Phi
, e.g., via convolution with a mollifier.

As consequences of Theorem
1
, we further obtain embedding relations, or even equality, between warped coorbit spaces and important families of (semi-)classical smoothness spaces. In particular, we show that certain warped coorbit spaces coincide with
\alpha
-modulation spaces spaces
[
15
,
2
]
and Besov-type spaces of dominating mixed smoothness
[
27
,
28
,
40
]
. For inhomogeneous Besov spaces, we show that in dimension
d=1
, they coincide with certain warped coorbit spaces as well. For
d>1
, we show a certain closeness relation and that equality cannot be achieved. Specifically, we obtain the following results, proven in Sections
7
–
9
and using the notion of
radial warping functions
. The radial warping function
\Phi_{\varrho}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
induced by a smooth, antisymmetric diffeomorphism
\varrho\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is given by
\Phi_{\varrho}(\xi)=\tfrac{\xi}{|\xi|}\cdot\varrho(|\xi|)
, see Section
5
and
[
19
, Section 8]
for details. The result uses a
slow start construction
that linearizes
\varrho
in a small neighborhood of the origin, but leaves it otherwise unchanged. This construction is introduced in Section
5
, Theorem
5.6
,

Theorem 2
.

For
\alpha\in(-\infty,1)
, let
\varrho_{\alpha}
be an arbitrary
slow start version
of
\varsigma_{\alpha}\colon[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty),\ \xi\mapsto(1+|\xi|)^{1-\alpha}-1
and
\varrho_{1}
a slow start version of
\varsigma_{1}\colon[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty),\ \xi\mapsto\ln(1+|\xi|)
. Then, the following hold for all
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
:

(1)

For
\alpha\in(-\infty,1)
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\operatorname{Co}(\Phi_{\varrho_{\alpha}},\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa})=M_{p,q}^{s,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,,\quad\text{with}\quad\kappa:=\kappa^{(\alpha,s,d,q)}\,\colon\,\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow[0,\infty),\ \xi\mapsto(1+|\xi|)^{s-d\alpha(q^{-1}-2^{-1})},

up to canonical identifications. Here,
M_{p,q}^{s,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the
\alpha
-modulation space with exponents
p,q
and weight parameter
s
.

(2)

If
\Phi_{\varrho_{1}}\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the
1
-dimensional radial warping function associated with
\varrho_{1}
and

\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d},\ \xi\mapsto(\Phi_{\varrho_{1}}(\xi_{1}),\ldots,\Phi_{\varrho_{1}}(\xi_{d})),

then we have, for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
,

S^{s}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\operatorname{Co}(\Phi,\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa})\,,\quad\text{with}\quad\kappa\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+},\xi=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{d})\mapsto\prod_{\ell=1}^{d}(1+|\xi_{\ell}|)^{s_{\ell}+2^{-1}-q^{-1}}\,,

up to canonical identifications. Here,
S^{s}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the Besov-type space of dominating mixed smoothness with exponents
p,q
and weight parameter
s
.

(3)

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
d\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\Phi_{\varrho_{1}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the
d
-dimensional radial warping function associated with
\varrho_{1}
. We have, for all
\varepsilon>0
, and with
\kappa^{(s,d,q)}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+},\ \xi\mapsto(1+|\xi|)^{s+d(2^{-1}-q^{-1})}
,

\operatorname{Co}(\Phi_{\varrho_{1}},\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa^{(s+\varepsilon,d,q)}})\hookrightarrow B_{s}^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Co}(\Phi_{\varrho_{1}},\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa^{(s-\varepsilon,d,q)}}),

up to canonical identifications. Here,
B_{s}^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the inhomogeneous Besov space with exponents
p,q
and weight parameter
s
. If and only if
d=1
, or
p=q=2
, we have the equality

B_{s}^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\operatorname{Co}(\Phi_{\varrho_{1}},\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa^{(s,q)}}).

The formal definition of the spaces
M_{p,q}^{s,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
S^{s}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
B_{s}^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is given in Sections
7
,
8
and
9
. Finally, we obtain conditions for equality of warped coorbit spaces and embeddings between warped coorbit spaces with respect to radial warping functions, in Section
6
.

Theorem 3
.

Let
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}\colon D\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfy the conditions of Theorem
1
and let
\kappa:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
satisfy the conditions of Theorem
1
with
\Phi=\Phi_{2}
. Then the following hold:

(1)

Suppose that there is a constant
C>0
, such that

\|\mathrm{D}\Phi_{3-j}(\Phi_{j}^{-1}(\xi))\cdot\mathrm{D}\Phi_{j}^{-1}(\xi)\|\leq C,\quad\text{for all }\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ and }j\in\{1,2\}.

Then we have, for all
p,q\in[1,\infty]
, the equality
\operatorname{Co}(\Phi_{1},\mathbf{L}_{\kappa}^{p,q})=\operatorname{Co}(\Phi_{2},\mathbf{L}_{\kappa}^{p,q})
.

(2)

Suppose that
\Phi_{1}=\Phi_{\varrho_{1}},\Phi_{2}=\Phi_{\varrho_{2}}
are radial, and that there are a constant
C>0
and a function
C_{\varrho_{1}}:[1,\infty)\rightarrow[1,\infty)
, such that

\varrho_{2}^{\prime}(\xi)\leq C\varrho_{1}^{\prime}(\xi),\quad\text{and}\quad C_{\varrho_{1}}(\alpha)^{-1}\varrho_{1}^{\prime}(\xi)\leq\varrho_{1}^{\prime}(\alpha\xi)\leq C_{\varrho_{1}}(\alpha)\varrho_{1}^{\prime}(\xi),\quad\text{for all }\alpha\in[1,\infty),\ \xi\in\mathbb{R}^{+},

then we have, for all
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
, the embeddings

\operatorname{Co}(\Phi_{\varrho_{1}},\mathbf{L}_{\kappa_{\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2},-\tilde{t}}}^{p,q})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Co}(\Phi_{\varrho_{2}},\mathbf{L}_{\kappa}^{p,q})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Co}(\Phi_{\varrho_{1}},\mathbf{L}_{\kappa_{\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2},t}}^{p,q}),

with
\tilde{t}=\max(0,\max(p^{-1},1-p^{-1})-q^{-1})\leq 1
, and
t=\max(0,q^{-1}-\min(p^{-1},1-p^{-1}))\leq 1
. Here, for
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
\kappa_{\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2},t}=\kappa\cdot\left[\frac{w_{2}\circ\Phi_{\varrho_{2}}}{w_{1}\circ\Phi_{\varrho_{1}}}\right]^{\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{2}-t}\quad\text{and}\quad w_{j}=\det(\mathrm{D}\Phi^{-1}_{\varrho_{j}}),\quad j\in\{1,2\}
.

We now proceed to introduce fundamental notation used in the paper and, in Section
2
, recall essential results on warped time-frequency systems, warping functions, and their associated coorbit spaces, as well as fundamental notions and results from the theory of decomposition spaces. Note that some results of potential interest, but not crucial to our main contributions, are deferred to the appendices.

1.2.
Notation and fundamental definitions

We write
\mathbb{R}^{+}=(0,\infty)
for the set of positive real numbers.
For the composition of functions
f
and
g
we use the notation
f\circ g=f(g(\bullet))
.
For a subset
M\subset X
of a fixed base set
X

(which is usually understood from the context), we use the indicator function

{\mathds{1}}_{M}
of the set
M
, where
{\mathds{1}}_{M}(x)=1
if
x\in M

and
{\mathds{1}}_{M}(x)=0
otherwise.

The (topological) dual space of a (complex) topological vector space
X

(i.e., the space of all continuous linear functionals
\varphi:X\to\mathbb{C}
)
is denoted by
X^{\prime}
, while the (topological)
anti
-dual of a Banach space
X

(i.e., the space of all
anti-linear
continuous functionals on
X
)
is denoted by
X^{\urcorner}
.

We use the convenient short-hand notations
\lesssim
and
\asymp
,
where
A\lesssim B
means
A\leq C\cdot B
, for some constant
C>0
that depends
on quantities that are either explicitly mentioned or clear from the context.

A\asymp B
means
A\lesssim B
and
B\lesssim A
.

1.2.1.
Norms and related notation

We write
|x|
for the Euclidean norm of a vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
and we denote the operator norm of a linear operator
T:X\to Y

by
\|T\|_{X\to Y}
, or by
\|T\|
, if
X,Y
are clear from the context.
In the expression
\|A\|
, a matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
is interpreted
as a linear map
(\mathbb{R}^{d},|\bullet|)\to(\mathbb{R}^{n},|\bullet|)
.
The open (Euclidean) ball around
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
of radius
r>0
is denoted by
B_{r}(x)
.

1.2.2.
Fourier-analytic notation and function spaces

The Lebesgue measure of a (measurable) subset
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is denoted by
\mu(M)
.
The Fourier transform is given by

\widehat{f}(\xi)=\mathcal{F}f(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)\,e^{-2\pi i\langle x,\xi\rangle}\,dx
,
for all
f\in\mathbf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
It is well-known that
\mathcal{F}
extends to a unitary automorphism of
\mathbf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
The inverse Fourier transform is denoted by
\widecheck{f}:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}f
.
We write
\mathbf{L}^{2,\mathcal{F}}(D):=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mathbf{L}^{2}(D))
for the space of
square-integrable functions whose Fourier transform vanishes (a.e.) outside of
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
In addition to the Fourier transform, the
modulation
and

translation operators

\mathbf{M}_{\omega}f=f\cdot e^{2\pi i\langle\omega,\bullet\rangle}
and

\mathbf{T}_{y}f=f(\bullet-y)
,
will be used frequently.

We write
\mathcal{D}(D)=\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(D)
for the space of test functions on an open set
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, i.e., the space of infinitely
differentiable functions with compact support in
D
. We denote by
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(D)
the space of distributions on
D
,
i.e., the topological dual space of
\mathcal{D}(D)
. For details regarding the topology on
\mathcal{D}(D)
, we refer to

[
32
, Chapter 6]
. We also consider the space
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
of Schwartz functions and its topological dual
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the space of tempered distributions. As is well-known (see e.g.
[
10
, Corollary 8.28]
),
the Fourier transform extends to a linear homeomorphism
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
and by duality also to a homeomorphism
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, where by definition

\langle\mathcal{F}\varphi,f\rangle_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}}=\langle\varphi,\mathcal{F}f\rangle_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}}
, cf.
[
10
, Page 295]
.

We consider a fixed open set
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and consistently write
\Lambda=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times D
. The weighted,

mixed
Lebesgue spaces
\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa}(\Lambda)
,
for
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
, consist of all measurable functions
F:\Lambda\to\mathbb{C}

for which

(1.2)

\|F\|_{\mathbf{L}_{\kappa}^{p,q}}:=\left\|\vphantom{\sum}\lambda_{2}\mapsto\left\|(\kappa\cdot F)(\bullet,\lambda_{2})\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{q}(D)}<\infty.

Here,
\kappa:\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
is a (measurable) weight function. Likewise, for a discrete set
I
and a weight
u=(u_{i})_{i\in I}
,
\ell^{q}(I)
denotes the space of all sequences
(c_{i})_{i\in I}
, such that the norm
\|(c_{i})_{i\in I}\|_{\ell^{q}_{u}}:=\|(u_{i}\cdot c_{i})_{i\in I}\|_{\ell^{q}}
is finite.

1.2.3.
Matrix notation

For matrix-valued functions
A:U\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, the notation

A(x)\langle y\rangle:=A(x)\cdot y
denotes the multiplication of the matrix
A(x)
,
x\in U
,
with the vector
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
in the usual sense.
Likewise, for a set
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we write

A(x)\langle M\rangle:=\big\{A(x)\langle y\rangle~:~y\in M\big\}.

Here, as in the remainder of the paper, the notation
A^{T}

denotes the transpose of a matrix
A
. Further, for matrices
A_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}\times d_{i}}
with
d_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
, for

i=1,\ldots,n
, we denote by

\mathrm{diag}(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},\quad\text{with}\quad d=d_{1}+\cdots+d_{n},

the block-diagonal matrix with blocks
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}
, in the indicated order.

1.2.4.
Convention for variables

Throughout this article,
x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
will be used to denote variables in time/position space,

\xi,\omega,\eta\in D
in frequency space,
\lambda,\rho,{\nu}\in\Lambda=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times D\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}
in phase space,
and finally
\sigma,\tau,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
denote variables in warped frequency space.
Unless otherwise stated, this also holds for subscript-indexed variants;
precisely, subscript indices (i.e.,
x_{i}
) are used to denote the
i
-th element
of a vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
In some cases, we also use subscripts to enumerate multiple vectors,
e.g.,
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
In this case, we denote the components of
x_{i}
by
(x_{i})_{j}
.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section we collect definitions and results from the literature that will be subsequently used. We begin by recalling the cornerstones of the theory of warped time-frequency systems in
[
19
]
.

2.1.
Warped time-frequency systems

Definition 2.1
.

Let
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open.
A
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism
\Phi:D\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
is called
a
warping function
, if
\det(\mathrm{D}\Phi^{-1}(\tau))>0

for all
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and if and the associated weight function

(2.1)

w:\quad\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+},\quad w(\tau)=\det(\mathrm{D}\Phi^{-1}(\tau)),

is
w_{0}
-moderate for some submultiplicative weight
w_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
, i.e.,
w(\tau+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})\leq w_{0}(\tau)\cdot w({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})
and
w_{0}(\tau+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})\leq w_{0}(\tau)\cdot w_{0}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})
, for all
\tau,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

If
0\neq\theta\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then the

(continuous) warped time-frequency system
generated by
\theta

and
\Phi
is the collection of functions

\mathcal{G}(\theta,\Phi):=(g_{y,\omega})_{(y,\omega)\in\Lambda}
, where

(2.2)

g_{y,\omega}:=\mathbf{T}_{y}\widecheck{g_{\omega}},\quad\text{ with }\quad g_{\omega}:=w(\Phi(\omega))^{-1/2}\cdot(\mathbf{T}_{\Phi(\omega)}\theta)\circ\Phi\text{ for all }y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\ \omega\in D.

Here, the function
g_{\omega}:D\to\mathbb{C}
is extended by zero to a
function on all of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, so that
\widecheck{g_{\omega}}
is well-defined. The associated

voice transform
is defined by

(2.3)

V_{\theta,\Phi}f:\quad\mathbb{R}^{d}\times D\to\mathbb{C},\quad(y,\omega)\mapsto\langle f,g_{y,\omega}\rangle,\text{ for all }f\in\mathbf{L}^{2,\mathcal{F}}(D).

It was established in
[
19
, Theorem 3.5]
that
\mathcal{G}(\theta,\Phi)
is a
continuous tight frame, with the frame bound given by
\|\theta\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}}^{2}<\infty
and that the map
(y,\omega)\mapsto g_{y,\omega}
is continuous. Note that we introduce the restriction to
\theta\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for technical reasons, while in
[
19
]
, warped time-frequency systems were defined under the weaker assumption
\theta\in\mathbf{L}^{2}_{\sqrt{w}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\supset\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Finally, it was noted in
[
19
, Section 3]
, that

(2.4)

w(\Phi(\xi))=[\det(D\Phi(\xi)]^{-1},\quad\text{for all }\xi\in D.

For warping functions that satisfy a mild regularity condition, it was shown that
\Phi
induces a family of coorbit spaces that is independent of the particular choice of
\theta\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset\mathbf{L}^{2}_{\sqrt{w}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We briefly recall these results in the detail required for this work.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\varnothing\neq D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open set and fix an integer
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
A map
\Phi:D\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a
k
-admissible warping function

with
control weight

v_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow[1,\infty)
,
if
v_{0}
is continuous, submultiplicative and radially increasing
and
\Phi
satisfies the following assumptions:

•

\Phi
is a
C^{k+1}
-diffeomorphism and
A:=\mathrm{D}\Phi^{-1}
has positive determinant.

•

With

(2.5)

\phi_{\tau}\left({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}\right):=\left(A^{-1}(\tau)A({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}+\tau)\right)^{T}=A^{T}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}+\tau)\cdot A^{-T}(\tau),

we have

(2.6)

\left\|\partial^{\alpha}\phi_{\tau}\left({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}\right)\right\|\leq v_{0}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})\qquad\text{ for all }\tau,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ and all multiindices }\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d},~\left|\alpha\right|\leq k.

Remark 2.3
.

We say that a
function
v:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is
radially increasing
, if

|x|\leq|y|\quad\text{ implies }\quad v(x)\leq v(y),\quad\text{ for all }x,y\in\Lambda.

In particular,
v
is radial, i.e.,
v(x)=v(y)
if
|x|=|y|
.
The requirement that the control weight
v_{0}
be radially increasing
leads to significant simplifications and simple computations show that this is no restriction.

Here, we are specifically interested in coorbit spaces with respect to weighted, mixed Lebesgue spaces, i.e.,
\operatorname{Co}(\mathcal{G}(\theta,\Phi),\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa})
. Unless the weight
\kappa\colon\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
is of no consequence for the desired result, we will from now on restrict to warping functions
\Phi\colon D\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
and weights
\kappa
that are compatible in the sense of the following definition. In particular
\kappa(\lambda)
and
v_{\Phi,\kappa}(\lambda)
defined below are independent of the first component of
\lambda=(x,\xi)\in\Lambda
, and we will commit the convenient abuse of notation of interpreting
\kappa
and
v_{\Phi,\kappa}
as
functions on
D
, i.e.,
\kappa:D\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

Definition 2.4
.

We say that the tuple

(\Phi,\kappa)
, with
\Phi\colon D\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\kappa\colon D\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
forms a
compatible pair
, or that
\Phi
and
\kappa
are
compatible
, if the following hold:

•

The set
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is open and
\Phi\colon D\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-diffeomorphism and a
(d+1)
-admissible warping function, with control weight
v_{0}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow[1,\infty)
.

•

The
transplanted weight

\kappa_{\Phi}:=\kappa\circ\Phi^{-1}
is
\tilde{\kappa}
-moderate for some continuous, submultiplicative and radially increasing weight
\tilde{\kappa}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[1,\infty)
.

If
\Phi
and
\kappa
are compatible, and
v_{0}
is a control weight for
\Phi
, then we define the weight
v_{\Phi,\kappa}\colon\Lambda\rightarrow[1,\infty)
by

(2.7)

v_{\Phi,\kappa}(x,\xi)=\tilde{v}(\Phi(\xi)),\quad\text{for all }(x,\xi)\in\Lambda,\quad\text{and with }\tilde{v}:=\tilde{\kappa}\cdot v_{0}^{d}.

The following theorem summarizes
[
19
, Theorem 2.13, Lemma 4.9, Theorem 7.1]
in the form required here.

Theorem 2.5

(
[
19
]
)
.

Assume that
\Phi\colon D\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\kappa\colon D\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
are compatible and let
v_{0}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow[1,\infty)
be a control weight for
\Phi
and
v:=v_{\Phi,\kappa}\colon\Lambda\rightarrow[1,\infty)
given by (
2.7
). For arbitrary
0\neq\theta\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the following hold:

(1)

The moderating weight
w_{0}
in Definition
2.1
can be chosen as
w_{0}=v_{0}^{d}
.

(2)

The space

(2.8)

\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1}:=\{f\in\mathbf{L}^{2,\mathcal{F}}(D)\colon\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1}}<\infty\},\text{ with }\|\bullet\|_{\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1}}\colon f\mapsto\|V_{\theta,\Phi}f\|_{\mathbf{L}^{1}(\Lambda)}

is a well-defined Banach space satisfying
\mathcal{G}(\theta,\Phi)\subset\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1}
.
It is independent of
\theta
in the sense that different choices yield equivalent norms.

(3)

The space

(2.9)

\operatorname{Co}(\mathcal{G}(\theta,\Phi),\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa}):=\{f\in(\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1})^{\urcorner}\colon\|f\|_{\operatorname{Co}(\Phi,\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa})}<\infty\},\text{ with }\|\bullet\|_{\operatorname{Co}(\Phi,\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa})}\colon f\mapsto\|V_{\theta,\Phi}f\|_{\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa}(\Lambda)}

is a well-defined Banach function space for all
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
. For any two nonzero
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have
\operatorname{Co}(\mathcal{G}(\theta_{1},\Phi),\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa})=\operatorname{Co}(\mathcal{G}(\theta_{2},\Phi),\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa})
, justifying the notation
\operatorname{Co}(\Phi,\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa}):=\operatorname{Co}(\mathcal{G}(\theta,\Phi),\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa})
.

Note that Item (1) holds for any
(d+1)
-admissible warping function, and
[
19
, Theorem 2.13]
can be applied since all its assumptions are covered by the conditions of Theorem
2.5
.

Remark 2.6
.

A closer inspection of the proof of
[
19
, Theorem 7.1]
shows that any weight
v\gtrsim v_{\Phi,\kappa}
can be used in place of
v_{\Phi,\kappa}
in Theorem
2.5
, so long as
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}v(x,\Phi^{-1}(\bullet))
is moderate with respect to some submultiplicative weight. This is clearly the case for
v_{\Phi,\kappa}
itself, since
\tilde{v}
in (
2.7
) is submultiplicative. In practice,
v
is usually chosen such that
(\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1})^{\urcorner}
is large enough to cover all coorbit spaces of interest. As long as that is the case, coorbit spaces
\operatorname{Co}(\Phi,\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa})
are independent of the specific weight
v
used to define the reservoir
(\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1})^{\urcorner}
, see
[
22
, Lemma 2.26]
.

2.2.
Decomposition spaces and coverings

In a nutshell, decomposition spaces are a generalization of Besov and modulation spaces.
The main difference is that instead of the usual dyadic covering used for Besov spaces, or the uniform covering used for modulation spaces, one can pick any covering that satisfies mild regularity conditions.
Such coverings will be called
decomposition coverings
, see Definition
2.7
. In particular, every decomposition
covering
\mathcal{Q}=(Q_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
can be equipped with
a
bounded admissible partition of unity (BAPU)

\Phi=(\varphi_{i})_{i\in I}

subordinate to the covering. For in-depth discussions of decomposition spaces,
we recommend the papers
[
7
,
3
,
36
,
37
]
.

Definition 2.7
.

(cf.
[
36
, Definition 2.5]
,
inspired by
[
5
, Definition 2.2]
and
[
3
, Definitions 2 and 7]
)

Let
\varnothing\neq\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open set.

(1)

A family
\mathcal{Q}=(Q_{i})_{i\in I}
of subsets of
\mathcal{O}

is called an
admissible covering
of
\mathcal{O}
,
if we have
\mathcal{O}=\bigcup_{i\in I}Q_{i}
and if
\sup_{i\in I}|i^{\ast}|<\infty
,
where the set
i^{\ast}
of
neighbors
of
i\in I
is given by

i^{\ast}:=\{j\in I\,:\,Q_{j}\cap Q_{i}\neq\varnothing\}
for
i\in I
.

(2)

An admissible covering
\mathcal{Q}=(Q_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\mathcal{O}
is called

semi-structured
if there is, for each
i\in I
, some invertible
linear map
T_{i}\in\mathrm{GL}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, a translation
b_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and an
open subset
Q_{i}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
the following properties hold:

•

We have
Q_{i}=T_{i}Q_{i}^{\prime}+b_{i}
for all
i\in I
,

•

There is some
R>0
satisfying

Q_{i}^{\prime}\subset\overline{B_{R}(0)}
for all
i\in I
.

•

There is some
C>0
satisfying

\|T_{i}^{-1}T_{\ell}\|\leq C\qquad\forall i\in I\text{ and all }\ell\in i^{\ast}.

(3)

An admissible covering
\mathcal{Q}=(Q_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\mathcal{O}

is called a
decomposition covering

if there exists a

bounded admissible partition of unity for
\mathcal{Q}
(
\mathcal{Q}
-BAPU)
.
A family
(\varphi_{i})_{i\in I}\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{O})
is called a
\mathcal{Q}
-BAPU,
if it satisfies the following properties:

•

\mathop{\operatorname{supp}}\varphi_{i}\subset Q_{i}
for all
i\in I
,

•

\sum_{i\in I}\varphi_{i}\equiv 1
on
\mathcal{O}
, and

•

\exists\ C>0\colon\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}\varphi_{i}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{1}}\leq C\qquad\forall\,i\in I
.

(4)

A semi-structured covering

\mathcal{Q}=(Q_{i})_{i\in I}=(T_{i}Q_{i}^{\prime}+b_{i})_{i\in I}
is called

tight
, if there is some
\varepsilon>0
and, for each
i\in I
, some

c_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfying
B_{\varepsilon}(c_{i})\subset Q_{i}^{\prime}
.

(5)

A weight
u=(u_{i})_{i\in I}
on the index set
I
of

\mathcal{Q}=(Q_{i})_{i\in I}
is called
\mathcal{Q}
-moderate
,
if there is a constant
C>0
satisfying

(2.10)

u_{i}\leq C\cdot u_{\ell}\qquad\forall i\in I\text{ and all }\ell\in i^{\ast}.

Remark 2.8
.

For later use, we also introduce the following generalization
of the sets
i^{\ast}
:
For
L\subset I
, we set
L^{\ast}:=\bigcup_{i\in L}i^{\ast}
.
Then, we inductively define the sets
L^{n\ast}
by
L^{1\ast}:=L^{\ast}

and
L^{(n+1)\ast}:=(L^{n\ast})^{\ast}
, in particular, we let
i^{n\ast}:=\{i\}^{n\ast}
.
Furthermore, we also set
Q_{L}:=\bigcup_{\ell\in L}Q_{\ell}
, for any sequence of sets
(Q_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{O}
.

The definition of decomposition spaces relies on a special reservoir space

Z^{\prime}(\mathcal{O})
, the topological dual of the space of functions whose Fourier transforms
are test functions.

Definition 2.9
.

For an open subset
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let

Z(\mathcal{O}):=\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{O}))=\left\{\widehat{f}\,:\,f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{O})\right\}\subset\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

and equip this space with the unique topology which makes

\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{O})\to Z(\mathcal{O})
a homeomorphism.
We denote the topological dual space of
Z(\mathcal{O})
by
Z^{\prime}(\mathcal{O})
,
and equip it with the weak-
\ast
-topology,
that is, with the topology of pointwise convergence on
Z(\mathcal{O})
.

Finally, we extend the Fourier transform by duality to
Z^{\prime}(\mathcal{O})
, that is,
we define

\mathcal{F}:Z^{\prime}(\mathcal{O})\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathcal{O}),f\mapsto f\circ\mathcal{F}

and write as usual
\widehat{f}:=\mathcal{F}f
for
f\in Z^{\prime}(\mathcal{O})
.
It is clear
\mathcal{F}
is an isomorphism

\mathcal{F}:Z^{\prime}(\mathcal{O})\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathcal{O})
, such that

Z^{\prime}(\mathcal{O})=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathcal{O}))
.

Definition 2.10
.

Given a decomposition covering
\mathcal{Q}=(Q_{i})_{i\in I}
of the open set
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
a
\mathcal{Q}
-moderate weight
{u=(u_{i})_{i\in I}}
, and integrability exponents

p,q\in[1,\infty]
, we define the decomposition space

\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{u}^{q}):=\left\{f\in Z^{\prime}(\mathcal{O})\,:\,\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{u}^{q})}<\infty\right\},

with

\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{u}^{q})}:=\left\|\left(\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\varphi_{i}\cdot\hat{f})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{p}}\right)_{i\in I}\right\|_{\ell_{u}^{q}}\in[0,\infty]\qquad\text{ for }f\in Z^{\prime}(\mathcal{O}).

Consider that
\hat{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathcal{O})
, such that
\varphi_{i}\cdot\hat{f}
is a distribution with compact support. Hence,

\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\varphi_{i}\cdot\hat{f})
is a smooth function by the Paley Wiener theorem,
see
[
32
, Theorem 7.23]
, and the
expression
\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\varphi_{i}\cdot\hat{f})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{p}}\in[0,\infty]

makes sense. One can show (see for instance

[
36
, Corollary 3.18, Lemma 4.13 and Theorem 3.21]
)
that the resulting space is independent of the chosen
\mathcal{Q}
-BAPU and
complete, i.e., a Banach space.

We further require criteria for comparing coverings. For this purpose, Feichtinger and
Gröbner
[
7
, Definition 3.3]
introduced the notions of
subordinate
,
equivalent
, and
moderate
coverings; see also Proposition 3.5 in the same
paper for equivalent reformulations of the notions introduced here.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}=(Q_{i})_{i\in I}
and
\mathcal{P}=(P_{j})_{j\in J}
be two admissible coverings of a set

\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

•

We say that
\mathcal{Q}
is
almost subordinate
to
\mathcal{P}
,
if there is some
N\in\mathbb{N}
and, for each
i\in I
, some

j=j_{i}\in J
with
Q_{i}\subset P_{{j}^{N\ast}}
, where the set

P_{{j}^{N\ast}}
is as in Remark
2.8
.

•

We say that
\mathcal{Q}
is
weakly subordinate
to
\mathcal{P}
, if we have

\sup_{i\in I}|\{j\in J\,:\,P_{j}\cap Q_{i}\neq\varnothing\}|<\infty.

•

We say that
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{P}
are
equivalent
if
\mathcal{Q}

is almost subordinate to
\mathcal{P}
and if also
\mathcal{P}
is almost
subordinate to
\mathcal{Q}
. Finally,
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{P}
are called

weakly equivalent
if
\mathcal{Q}
is weakly subordinate to
\mathcal{P}

and also
\mathcal{P}
is weakly subordinate to
\mathcal{Q}
.

Remark 2.12
.

Since
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
are admissible, weak subordinateness is clearly implied by almost subordinateness.
Furthermore, on the set of admissible coverings, the relation of being almost subordinate
is transitive, and the same is true of weak subordinateness,
cf.
[
7
, Proposition 3.5]
.
In particular, the relation defined by (weak) equivalence of admissible
coverings is an equivalence relation on the set of admissible coverings of
each fixed set
\mathcal{O}
.

The above notion of weak equivalence yields a condition for equality of decomposition spaces.
This result is essentially taken from
[
7
, Theorem 3.7]
;
a different proof is given in
[
36
, Lemma 6.11]
.
It turns out that it is in fact quite sharp,
see
[
36
, Theorem 6.9]
.

Theorem 2.13
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}=(Q_{i})_{i\in I}
and
\mathcal{P}=(P_{j})_{j\in J}
be two
decomposition coverings of the open set
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
and let
u=(u_{i})_{i\in I}
and
v=(v_{j})_{j\in J}
be
\mathcal{Q}
-moderate
and
\mathcal{P}
-moderate, respectively.

If
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{P}
are weakly equivalent and if there is
C>0

with
C^{-1}\cdot u_{i}\leq v_{j}\leq C\cdot u_{i}
for all
i\in I
and

j\in J
with
Q_{i}\cap P_{j}\neq\varnothing
, then we have

\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{u}^{q})=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{P},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{v}^{q})

with equivalent norms, for all
p,q\in[1,\infty]
.

It is often difficult to verify equivalence and almost subordinateness of two
coverings directly, even when their
weak
variants are easily shown.
Therefore, the following result, originally
[
7
, Proposition 3.6]
, is rather useful.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}=(Q_{i})_{i\in I}
and
\mathcal{P}=(P_{j})_{j\in J}
be two admissible
coverings of a connected subset
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that each
Q_{i}

is path-connected and such that each
P_{j}
is open.
Then
\mathcal{Q}
is almost subordinate to
\mathcal{P}
if and only if
\mathcal{Q}
is
weakly subordinate to
\mathcal{P}
.

Remark
.

In
[
7
, Proposition 3.6]
, it is claimed (under the same assumptions)
that
\mathcal{P}
is almost subordinate to
\mathcal{Q}
if and only if
\mathcal{P}
is
weakly subordinate to
\mathcal{Q}
. The proof, however, establishes the claim with
interchanged roles of
\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{P}
, that is, as stated above.

3.
The frequency-space coverings induced by a warping function
\Phi

In this section, we study the
(\delta,r)
-fine frequency covering induced by
\Phi
. Recall that

\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}=\big(\,Q_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}\,\big)_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}},\quad\text{with}\quad Q_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}:=\Phi^{-1}(\delta\cdot B_{r}(k)),

for
\delta,r>0
. It is immediate that the parameter
\delta>0
determines the “sampling density” (in frequency) of the covering, while
r
only influences the size of the individual elements of the covering and thus is a kind of “redundancy” parameter. We will now show that
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
indeed covers
D
, if
r
is large enough.

Lemma 3.1
.

\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
is a covering of
D
if and only if
r>\sqrt{d}/2
.

Proof.

Since
\Phi:D\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is bijective, the claim is equivalent to showing
that
\mathbb{R}^{d}=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\left(\delta\cdot B_{r}(k)\right)
if and only if

r>\sqrt{d}/2
.
Since
\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},\tau\mapsto\delta\tau
is bijective,
we can furthermore assume
\delta=1
.
The proof is completed by observing that

\max_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\min_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\tau-k|=\sqrt{d}/2
.
Indeed, the maximum is attained for
\tau=\frac{1}{2}\cdot(1,\dots,1)
.
∎

The next lemma shows that
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
,
\delta>0
,
r>\sqrt{d}/2
, is a semi-structured covering and all
(\delta,r)
-fine frequency coverings
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
are equivalent, provided that
\Phi
is at least
0
-admissible.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\Phi:D\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a
0
-admissible warping function, and let

\delta>0
and
r>\sqrt{d}/2
be arbitrary.
For
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we have

(3.1)

Q^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi,k}=T_{k}Q_{k}^{\prime}+b_{k},\quad\text{with }\quad T_{k}:=A(\delta k),\quad b_{k}:=\Phi^{-1}(\delta k)\quad\text{ and }\quad Q_{k}^{\prime}:=T_{k}^{-1}\langle Q^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi,k}-b_{k}\rangle.

The family

\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}=(Q^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi,k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}

is a semi-structured admissible covering of
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, in particular, it is admissible. Specifically,
Q_{k}^{\prime}\subset\overline{B_{R}(0)}
, for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, with
R=\delta r\cdot v_{0}(\delta r\cdot e_{1})
.
Furthermore, for arbitrary
r,\rho>\sqrt{d}/2
and
\varepsilon,\delta>0
,
the two coverings
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
and

\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\varepsilon,\rho)}
are equivalent in the sense of
Definition
2.11
.

If
v^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
is a
v
-moderate weight, for a submultiplicative weight
v:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
then the weight
\tilde{v}:=v^{\prime}\circ\Phi
has the following properties:

(1)

There are constants
C_{0},C_{1}>0
, such that
C_{0}\cdot\tilde{v}(\xi)\leq\tilde{v}(\omega)\leq C_{1}\cdot\tilde{v}(\xi)

for all
\xi\in Q^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi,k},\omega\in Q^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi,\ell}
, for all
\ell\in k^{\ast}
and all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

(2)

The sequence
(\tilde{v}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
,
\tilde{v}_{k}:=\tilde{v}(y_{k})
, for some arbitrary
y_{k}\in Q^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi,k}
, is
\mathcal{Q}^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi}
-moderate.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.1
,
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
is a covering of
D
for all
r>\sqrt{d}/2
.
Note that
Q_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}
is path-connected and open for all
\delta,r>0
, such that, by Lemma
2.14
, weak equivalence of of
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\varepsilon,\rho)}
implies equivalence. Moreover, admissibility of
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
is the same as weak equivalence to itself. By symmetry, it suffices to show weak subordinateness of
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
to
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\varepsilon,\rho)}
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
be arbitrary. Then, each
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with

Q_{\Phi,\ell}^{(\varepsilon,\rho)}\cap Q_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}\neq\varnothing

satisfies

\varnothing\neq[\varepsilon\cdot B_{\rho}(\ell)]\cap[\delta\cdot B_{r}(k)]
,
and thus

\varnothing\neq B_{\rho}(\ell)\cap\left(\varepsilon^{-1}\delta\cdot B_{r}(k)\right)=B_{\rho}(\ell)\cap B_{\delta r/\varepsilon}(\delta k/\varepsilon),

so that
|\ell-\delta k/\varepsilon|<\rho+\frac{\delta r}{\varepsilon}=:\tilde{r}
.
Thus, if we fix some
\ell_{0}=\ell_{0}(k)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with

Q_{\Phi,\ell_{0}}^{(\varepsilon,\rho)}\cap Q_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}\neq\varnothing
,
then every
\ell
as above satisfies
|\ell-\ell_{0}|<2\tilde{r}
,
and thus
\ell\in\ell_{0}+\{-R,\dots,R\}^{d}
for
R:=\lceil 2\tilde{r}\rceil
.
We have thus shown

\left|\left\{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\,:\,Q_{\Phi,\ell}^{(\varepsilon,\rho)}\cap Q_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}\neq\varnothing\right\}\right|\leq|\left\{-R,\dots,R\right\}^{d}|=(1+2R)^{d}

for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Hence,
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}

is weakly subordinate to
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\varepsilon,\rho)}
.

The representation of
Q^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi,k}
as per Equation (
3.1
) is obvious. For
\mathcal{Q}^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi}
to be semi-structured, it remains to show that
\|A(\delta k)^{-1}A(\delta\ell)\|\leq C
, for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,
\ell\in k^{\ast}
and some
C>0
, as well as
Q_{k}^{\prime}\subset\overline{B_{R}(0)}
, for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and some
R>0
.

Since
\Phi
is
0
-admissible, Equation (
2.6
) implies
\|A(\delta k)^{-1}A(\delta\ell)\|\leq v_{0}(\delta(k-\ell))\leq v_{0}(2\delta r\cdot e_{1})
, for all
k,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Here we used
|\ell-k|<2r
for all
\ell\in k^{\ast}
similar to above. Finally, if we express the directional derivative of
\Phi^{-1}
through its Jacobian, we obtain, for all
\tau,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(3.2)

\begin{split}\left|[(D\Phi^{-1})({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})]^{-1}\langle\Phi^{-1}(\tau)-\Phi^{-1}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})\rangle\right|&=\left|[(D\Phi^{-1})({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})]^{-1}\int_{0}^{1}(D\Phi^{-1})({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}+s(\tau-{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}))\left\langle\tau-{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}\right\rangle~ds\right|\\
&\leq|\tau-{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}|\cdot\max_{s\in[0,1]}\left\|[(D\Phi^{-1})({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})]^{-1}(D\Phi^{-1})({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}+s(\tau-{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}))\right\|\\
&=|\tau-{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}|\cdot\max_{s\in[0,1]}\left\|\phi_{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}+s(\tau-{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}))\right\|.\end{split}

If
{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}=\delta k
and
\tau\in\delta\cdot B_{r}(k)
, then we conclude that

|[(D\Phi^{-1})({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})]^{-1}\langle\Phi^{-1}(\tau)-\Phi^{-1}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})\rangle|<\delta r\cdot v_{0}(\delta r\cdot e_{1}).

Hence,
Q_{k}^{\prime}=A(\delta k)^{-1}\langle\Phi^{-1}(\delta\cdot B_{r}(k))-\Phi^{-1}(\delta k)\rangle\subset B_{R}(0)\subset\overline{B_{R}(0)}
, with
R=\delta r\cdot v_{0}(\delta r\cdot e_{1})
, independent of
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
is semi-structured.

Finally, we prove the claims regarding the weight
\tilde{v}
: To prove (1), observe that

\tau\in Q^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi,k}
and
{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}\in Q^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi,\ell}
with
\ell\in k^{\ast}

implies
|\Phi(\tau)-\Phi({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})|<4\delta r
,
so that
v
-moderateness of
v^{\prime}
yields

\tilde{v}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})=v^{\prime}(\Phi({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}))\leq v^{\prime}(\Phi(\tau))v(\Phi({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})-\Phi(\tau))\leq\left[\sup_{\tau_{0}\in{B_{4\delta r}(0)}}v(\tau_{0})\right]\cdot\tilde{v}(\tau)=\left[\max_{\tau_{0}\in\overline{B_{4\delta r}(0)}}v(\tau_{0})\right]\cdot\tilde{v}(\tau).

Interchange the roles of
\tau
and

Υ

and set
C_{0}^{-1}:=C_{1}:=\max_{\tau_{0}\in\overline{B_{4\delta r}(0)}}v(\tau_{0})
to complete the proof of Item (1). Item (2) is a direct consequence of (1); more specifically,
the constant
C
in (
2.10
) can be chosen as
C_{1}
.
∎

One might expect that the existence of
r>0
such that
\Phi^{-1}({B_{r}(x_{k})})\subset Q^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi,k}
, for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, implies tightness of
\mathcal{Q}^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi}
. This is indeed the case, but we require some auxiliary results to prove the statement. In particular, we need a fundamental relation between the sizes of
\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(\xi)+B_{\vartheta}(0))
and

D\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(\xi))\left\langle B_{\vartheta}(0)\right\rangle
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\Phi:D\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a
1
-admissible warping function with
control weight
v_{0}
.
Then we have

\|\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}-A^{-1}(\tau_{0})\,A(\tau)\|=\|\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}-A^{T}(\tau)\,A^{-T}(\tau_{0})\|\leq d\cdot v_{0}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|\cdot e_{1})\cdot\|\tau-\tau_{0}\|_{\infty}\,,

for all
\tau,\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proof.

The first identity simply follows from
\|B^{T}\|=\|B\|
for

B\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
.
Next, by definition of
\phi_{\tau_{0}}
(see Equation (
2.5
)),
we have

\phi_{\tau_{0}}(\tau-\tau_{0})=A^{T}\big((\tau-\tau_{0})+\tau_{0}\big)\,A^{-T}(\tau_{0})=A^{T}(\tau)\,A^{-T}(\tau_{0})
,
and
\phi_{\tau_{0}}(0)=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}-A^{T}(\tau)\,A^{-T}(\tau_{0})\|=\|\phi_{\tau_{0}}(\tau-\tau_{0})-\phi_{\tau_{0}}(0)\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=t}\Big[\phi_{\tau_{0}}\big(s(\tau-\tau_{0})\big)\Big]\,dt\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}(\partial_{\ell}\,\phi_{\tau_{0}})\big(t(\tau-\tau_{0})\big)\cdot(\tau-\tau_{0})_{\ell}\,\,dt\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\left[|(\tau-\tau_{0})_{\ell}|\cdot\int_{0}^{1}\big\|(\partial_{\ell}\,\phi_{\tau_{0}})\big(t(\tau-\tau_{0})\big)\big\|\,dt\right]

\displaystyle\leq d\cdot v_{0}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|\cdot e_{1})\cdot\|\tau-\tau_{0}\|_{\infty}\,.

Here, we used in the last step that
v_{0}
is radially increasing, so that

v_{0}\big(t(\tau-\tau_{0})\big)\leq v_{0}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|\cdot e_{1})
,
and furthermore that
\|\partial_{\ell}\,\phi_{\tau_{0}}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})\|\leq v_{0}(t(\tau-\tau_{0}))
for all
t\in[0,1]
,
as a consequence of (
2.6
).
∎

We are now ready to compare
\Phi^{-1}(B_{\vartheta}(\Phi(\xi)))
to its
“linearization”

\xi+D\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(\xi))\langle B_{\vartheta}(\Phi(\xi))-\Phi(\xi)\rangle
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
\Phi:D\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a
1
-admissible warping function with control weight
v_{0}
.
Then, for every
0<\vartheta\leq\vartheta_{0}:=(2d\cdot v_{0}(1))^{-1}
and every
\xi\in D
, we have

(3.3)

\xi+D\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(\xi))\langle\overline{B_{\vartheta/4}(0)}\rangle\subset\Phi^{-1}\big(\Phi(\xi)+B_{\vartheta}(0)\big).

In particular,

\xi+D\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(\xi))\left\langle B_{\vartheta/4}(0)\right\rangle\subset D

for all
\xi\in D
.

Proof.

First of all, we define for fixed, but arbitrary
\xi\in D
the map

g\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},\ {\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}\mapsto{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}-A^{-1}(\Phi(\xi))\left\langle\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(\xi)+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})\right\rangle.

The Jacobian of
g
is given by

(3.4)

Dg({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})=\mathrm{id}-A^{-1}(\Phi(\xi))\cdot D\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(\xi)+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})=\mathrm{id}-A^{-1}(\Phi(\xi))\cdot A(\Phi(\xi)+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})\,.

In particular,
Dg(0)=0
.
For the moment, we assume that there is some
\vartheta_{0}>0
, such that

(3.5)

\sup_{{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}\in\overline{B_{\vartheta_{0}}(0)}}\|Dg({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})\|\leq 1/2.

With this estimate, proving Equation (
3.3
)
essentially amounts to repeating the usual proof of the inverse mapping
theorem
(see for instance
[
24
, Chapter XIV, Theorem 1.2]
).

Indeed, let us fix
0<\vartheta\leq\vartheta_{0}
.
We define

h_{\tau}\colon\overline{B_{\vartheta}(0)}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}\mapsto\tau-g(0)+g({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}),\ \qquad\text{for arbitrary}\qquad\tau\in\overline{B_{\vartheta/2}(0)}.

Note that
h_{\tau}(0)=\tau
and

\|Dh_{\tau}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})\|=\|Dg({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})\|\leq 1/2
, by
Equation (
3.5
).
The latter yields, using the fundamental theorem of calculus, that

|h_{\tau}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})-h_{\tau}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}^{\prime})|\leq\frac{1}{2}|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}-{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}^{\prime}|

for all
{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}},{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}^{\prime}\in\overline{B_{\vartheta}(0)}
, that is,

h_{\tau}
is a contraction.
Moreover,

|h_{\tau}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})|\leq|h_{\tau}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})-h_{\tau}(0)|+|h_{\tau}(0)|\leq\frac{1}{2}|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}-0|+|\tau|\leq\vartheta,\quad\text{ for all }{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}\in\overline{B_{\vartheta}(0)},

so that
h_{\tau}:\overline{B_{\vartheta}(0)}\to\overline{B_{\vartheta}(0)}

is a self-map.

As an application of Banach’s fixed point theorem
(see for instance
[
24
, Chapter XIV, Lemma 1.1]
),
there is thus a (unique)

{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{\tau}\in\overline{B_{\vartheta}}(0)
satisfying

h_{\tau}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{\tau})={\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{\tau}
.

Noting that
(A^{-1}(\Phi(\xi)))^{-1}=D\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(\xi))
, we obtain

\displaystyle{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{\tau}=h_{\tau}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{\tau})=\tau-g(0)+g({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{\tau})

\displaystyle=\tau+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{\tau}-\left(A^{-1}(\Phi(\xi))\langle\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(\xi)+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{\tau})\rangle-A^{-1}(\Phi(\xi))\langle\xi\rangle\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle A^{-1}(\Phi(\xi))\langle\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(\xi)+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{\tau})\rangle

\displaystyle=\tau+A^{-1}(\Phi(\xi))\langle\xi\rangle

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(\xi)+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{\tau})

\displaystyle=\xi+D\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(\xi))\left\langle\tau\right\rangle.

Consequently, since
\tau\in\overline{B_{\vartheta/2}(0)}
was arbitrary
and
{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{\tau}\in\overline{B_{\vartheta}(0)}
,
we see

\xi+D\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(\xi))\langle\overline{B_{\vartheta/2}(0)}\rangle\subset\Phi^{-1}\big(\,\Phi(\xi)+\overline{B_{\vartheta}(0)}\,\big),

for all
0<\vartheta\leq\vartheta_{0}
.
Applying the above with
\vartheta/2<\vartheta\leq\vartheta_{0}
instead of

\vartheta
itself yields the desired inclusion in Equation (
3.3
):

\xi+D\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(\xi))\left\langle\overline{B_{\vartheta/4}(0)}\right\rangle\subset\Phi^{-1}\big(\,\Phi(\xi)+\overline{B_{\vartheta/2}(0)}\,\big)\subset\Phi^{-1}\big(\Phi(\xi)+B_{\vartheta}(0)\big).

It remains to show that (
3.5
) indeed holds for
\vartheta_{0}:=(2d\cdot v_{0}(1))^{-1}
. But Lemma
3.3
implies that

\|\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}-A^{-1}(\tau_{0})\,A(\tau)\|\leq d\cdot v_{0}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|\cdot e_{1})\cdot|\tau-\tau_{0}|\quad\text{for all}\quad\tau,\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,.

Further note that
v_{0}
is radially increasing and thus
\vartheta_{0}\leq 1
, since

1\leq v_{0}(0)\leq v_{0}(1)
. Recalling the formula (
3.4
)
for the derivative of
g
, we see for
{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}\in\overline{B_{\vartheta_{0}}}(0)
that

\|Dg({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})\|=\|\mathrm{id}-A^{-1}(\Phi(\xi))\,A(\Phi(\xi)+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})\|\leq d\cdot v_{0}(|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}|\cdot e_{1})\cdot|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}|\leq d\cdot v_{0}(e_{1})\cdot|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}|\leq\frac{1}{2}\,,

as desired. Here, we used again that
v_{0}
is radially increasing, implying

v_{0}(|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}|)\leq v_{0}(1)
.∎

As the first consequence of Lemma
3.4
,
we can now show that the family
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}

is indeed a tight, semi-structured admissible covering of
D
,
if
r>\sqrt{d}/2
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\Phi:D\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a
1
-admissible warping function, and let

\delta>0
and
r>\sqrt{d}/2
be arbitrary. The semi-structured covering

\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}=(Q^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi,k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is tight.

Proof.

Let
\vartheta_{0}>0
as in
Lemma
3.4
, and set

\vartheta:=\min\{\delta r,\vartheta_{0}\}>0
.
Then, we apply Equation (
3.3
)
(with
\xi=\Phi^{-1}(\delta k)=b_{k}
) to derive

\displaystyle Q_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}=\Phi^{-1}(\delta\cdot B_{r}(k))

\displaystyle\supset\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(\xi)+B_{\vartheta}(0))

\displaystyle({\scriptstyle{\text{Equation }\eqref{eq:covering_linearization}}})

\displaystyle\supset\xi+D\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(\xi))\left\langle B_{\vartheta/4}(0)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=b_{k}+T_{k}\left\langle B_{\vartheta/4}(0)\right\rangle.

But this implies
Q_{k}^{\prime}\supset B_{\vartheta/4}(0)
.
Since this holds for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we see that

\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
is tight.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

Although not required in this work, it may sometimes be more convenient to consider a structured covering, in the sense of
[
36
, Definition 2.5]
. In Appendix
C
, we show that this is indeed possible, and there is a family of structured coverings equivalent to
\mathcal{Q}^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi}
, albeit for restricted ranges of the parameters
\delta,r
.

To ensure that the decomposition spaces
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q}^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell^{q}_{u})
are properly defined, we need to construct a
\mathcal{Q}^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi}
-BAPU, such that
\mathcal{Q}^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi}
is a decomposition covering. Such a construction is rather straightforward, but we now construct a
special BAPU that will be useful for proving equivalence of the
coorbit spaces generated by
\mathcal{G}(\theta,\Phi)
with the decomposition
spaces
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q}^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell^{q}_{u})
.

Definition 3.7
.

Fix some
\delta>0
,
r>\sqrt{d}/2
and some

0<\vartheta<r-\sqrt{d}/2
.
Let
\zeta\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be such that

(3.6)

\zeta\geq 0,\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\zeta(\tau)d\tau=1,\quad\text{ and }\quad K_{0}:=\mathop{\operatorname{supp}}\zeta\subset\delta\cdot B_{\vartheta}(0).

For all
\xi\in D
, define the functions

(3.7)

\zeta^{(\xi)}:=[w(\Phi(\xi))]^{-1/2}\cdot(\mathbf{T}_{\Phi(\xi)}\zeta)\circ\Phi\quad\text{ and }\quad\varphi^{(\xi)}:=[w(\Phi(\xi))]^{-1/2}\cdot\zeta^{(\xi)}.

Furthermore, with

M_{k}:=\Phi^{-1}\left(\delta\cdot\left(k+\smash{\left[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)^{d}}\vphantom{\left[\frac{1}{2}\right)}\,\right)\right)
,

k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, define

(3.8)

\varphi_{k}:D\to[0,\infty),\eta\mapsto\int_{M_{k}}\varphi^{(\xi)}(\eta)\,d\xi.

The collection
(\varphi_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
will be shown to be the desired BAPU in Proposition
3.9
.
Note that
\varphi^{(\xi)}\in\mathcal{D}(D)\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

for each
\xi\in D
, provided that
\Phi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(D)
.
The next lemma is a crucial ingredient for proving the estimate

\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}\varphi_{k}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{1}}\leq C

for our
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
-BAPU.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
\Phi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(D)
be a
(d+1)
-admissible warping function and
\zeta\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as in Definition
3.7
. There is a constant
C>0
, depending on
\zeta
and the chosen control weight
v_{0}
for
\Phi
, satisfying

\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}\varphi^{(\xi)}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{1}}\leq C\cdot[w(\Phi(\xi))]^{-1}\quad\text{for all}\quad\xi\in D.

Proof.

Because of
\zeta\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
there is some
\widetilde{\zeta}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with

\widetilde{\zeta}\geq 0
and
\widetilde{\zeta}\equiv 1
on
\mathop{\operatorname{supp}}\zeta
.
Now, we set
\tau_{0}:=\Phi(\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and calculate

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}\varphi^{\left(\xi\right)}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{1}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\int_{D}\>\frac{1}{w\left(\Phi\left(\xi\right)\right)}\cdot\zeta\left(\Phi\left(\eta\right)-\Phi\left(\xi\right)\right)\cdot e^{2\pi i\left\langle y,\eta\right\rangle}\,d\eta\right|\,dy

\displaystyle\left({\scriptstyle{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}=\Phi\left(\eta\right)-\tau_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{w\left({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}+\tau_{0}\right)}{w\left(\tau_{0}\right)}\cdot\zeta\left({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}\right)\cdot e^{2\pi i\left\langle y,\Phi^{-1}\left({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}+\tau_{0}\right)\right\rangle}\,d{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}\right|\,dy

\displaystyle\left({\scriptstyle x=-A^{T}\left(\tau_{0}\right)\langle y\rangle\text{ and }\left|\det A^{T}\left(\tau_{0}\right)\right|=w\left(\tau_{0}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w\left(\tau_{0}\right)}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{w\left({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}+\tau_{0}\right)}{w\left(\tau_{0}\right)}\cdot\zeta\left({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}\right)\cdot e^{-2\pi i\left\langle A^{-T}\left(\tau_{0}\right)\langle x\rangle,\Phi^{-1}\left({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}+\tau_{0}\right)\right\rangle}\,d{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}\right|\,dx

\displaystyle=:\frac{1}{w(\tau_{0})}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F_{\tau_{0}}(x)\,dx.

Since
\zeta,\widetilde{\zeta}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we can invoke
[
19
, Theorem 4.8]
(with
\tau=0
) to obtain the existence of a finite constant
C=C(\zeta,v_{0})>0
, such that
F_{\tau_{0}}(x)\leq C\cdot(1+|x|)^{-(d+1)}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, uniformly over
\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We get

\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}\varphi^{(\xi)}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{1}}\leq C\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+|x|)^{-(d+1)}\,dx\cdot\frac{1}{w(\tau_{0})}=C^{\prime}\cdot\frac{1}{w(\Phi(\xi))},

as claimed.
∎

Proposition 3.9
.

If
\Phi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
is a
(d+1)
-admissible warping function, then the collection of functions
(\varphi_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
as given in Definition
3.7
is a BAPU for
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
.

Proof.

We leave to the reader the straightforward proof that
\varphi_{k}\in C^{\infty}
and begin by noting
for each measurable
M\subset D
and arbitrary
\eta\in D
, because of

[w(\Phi(\xi))]^{-1}=\det D\Phi(\xi)
for
\xi\in D
, (cf. Equation (
2.4
)) that

(3.9)

\displaystyle\varphi_{M}(\eta)

\displaystyle:=\int_{M}\varphi^{(\xi)}(\eta)\,d\xi

\displaystyle=\int_{M}[w(\Phi(\xi))]^{-1}\cdot\zeta(\Phi(\eta)-\Phi(\xi))\,d\xi

\displaystyle({\scriptstyle{\tau=\Phi(\eta)-\Phi(\xi)}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Phi(\eta)-\Phi(M)}\zeta(\tau)\,d\tau.

In particular, since
\Phi(\eta)-\Phi(D)=\Phi(\eta)-\mathbb{R}^{d}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and because of

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\zeta(\tau)d\tau=1
(see (
3.6
)),
we get
\varphi_{D}(\eta)=1
for all
\eta\in D
.
But it is not hard to see
D=\biguplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}M_{k}
, so that the monotone convergence theorem yields

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\varphi_{k}(\eta)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\varphi_{M_{k}}(\eta)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\int_{\Phi(\eta)-\Phi(M_{k})}\zeta(\tau)\,d\tau=\int_{\Phi(\eta)-\Phi(D)}\zeta(\tau)\,d\tau=\varphi_{D}(\eta)=1

for all
\eta\in D
.

Next, if
0\neq\varphi_{k}(\eta)=\varphi_{M_{k}}(\eta)
, then Equation (
3.9
)
shows that there is some
\tau\in\Phi(\eta)-\Phi(M_{k})
satisfying
\tau\in\mathop{\operatorname{supp}}\zeta=K_{0}
.
Hence,
\Phi(\eta)\in\tau+\Phi(M_{k})\subset K_{0}+\Phi(M_{k})
, so that
\eta\in\Phi^{-1}(K_{0}+\Phi(M_{k}))
.
Since the set on the right-hand side is compact, and since
\vartheta<r-\sqrt{d}/2
, so that

[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})^{d}+B_{\vartheta}(0)\subset\overline{B_{\sqrt{d}/2}(0)}+B_{\vartheta}(0)\subset B_{r}(0)
,
we conclude in view of (
3.6
) that

\mathop{\operatorname{supp}}\varphi_{k}\subset\Phi^{-1}(K_{0}+\Phi(M_{k}))\subset\Phi^{-1}\left(\delta\cdot B_{\vartheta}(0)+\delta\left(k+\smash{\left[-\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}},\frac{1}{2}\right)^{d}}\,\vphantom{\left[\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}\right]}\right)\right)\subset\Phi^{-1}\left(\delta\cdot(k+B_{r}(0))\right)=Q_{k}^{(\delta,r)},

as desired.

It remains to estimate
\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}\varphi_{k}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{1}}
. But Fubini’s theorem, the application of which will be justified post-hoc, shows

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}^{-1}\varphi_{k})(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{M_{k}}\varphi^{(\xi)}(\eta)\cdot e^{2\pi i\left\langle\eta,x\right\rangle}d\xi\,d\eta

\displaystyle=\int_{M_{k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi^{(\xi)}(\eta)\cdot e^{2\pi i\left\langle\eta,x\right\rangle}d\eta\,d\xi

\displaystyle=\int_{M_{k}}(\mathcal{F}^{-1}\varphi^{(\xi)})(x)\,d\xi,

so that Minkowski’s inequality for integrals (cf.
[
10
, Theorem 6.19]
)
and Lemma
3.8
yield

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}\varphi_{k}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{1}}\leq\int_{M_{k}}\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}\varphi^{(\xi)}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{1}}d\xi

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\int_{M_{k}}\frac{1}{w(\Phi(\xi))}d\xi

\displaystyle({\scriptstyle{\text{Equation}\eqref{eq:PhiJacobianDetExpressedInW}}})

\displaystyle=C\cdot\mu(\Phi(M_{k}))=C\cdot\mu\left(\delta(k+[\textstyle{-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}})^{d})\right)=C\cdot\delta^{d},

where the constant
C>0
is provided by Lemma
3.8
.
Since this estimate holds uniformly for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we are done, once we justify the application
of Fubini’s theorem above.
But if
\xi\in M_{k}
and
\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with

0\neq\varphi^{(\xi)}(\eta)=[w(\Phi(\xi))]^{-1}\cdot\zeta(\Phi(\eta)-\Phi(\xi))
, then

\Phi(\eta)-\Phi(\xi)\in\mathop{\operatorname{supp}}\zeta=K_{0}
,
so that
{\Phi(\eta)\in\Phi(\xi)+K_{0}\subset\overline{M_{k}}+K_{0}}
,
and hence
\eta\in\Phi^{-1}(\overline{M_{k}}+K_{0})=:K_{k}
,
with
K_{k}\subset D
compact.
Furthermore, using

|\varphi^{(\xi)}(\eta)|\leq\|\mathcal{F}\mathcal{F}^{-1}\varphi^{(\xi)}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{\infty}}\leq\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}\varphi^{(\xi)}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{1}}\leq C\cdot[w(\Phi(\xi))]^{-1}
(cf. Lemma
3.8
), we get
that

\int_{M_{k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\varphi^{(\xi)}(\eta)|d\eta\,d\xi\leq\int_{M_{k}}\mu(K_{k})\cdot\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}\varphi^{(\xi)}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{1}}d\xi\leq C\cdot\mu(K_{k})\cdot\int_{M_{k}}[w(\Phi(\xi))]^{-1}d\xi<\infty,

thereby justifying the application of Fubini’s theorem above.
∎

4.
Warped coorbit spaces as decomposition spaces

Recall that we always assume that the warping function
\Phi
and the weight
\kappa
are compatible, as per Definition
2.4
. In this setting, we will show

(4.1)

\operatorname{Co}(\Phi,\mathbf{L}_{\kappa}^{p,q})=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell^{q}_{u}),

up to trivial identifications. The weight
u=u^{(q)}=(u^{(q)}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
will be given by

(4.2)

u_{k}:=u^{(q)}_{k}:=\kappa(\Phi^{-1}(\delta k))\cdot[w(\delta k)]^{\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{2}}\quad\text{for all}\quad k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},

with
w=\det(D\Phi^{-1})
as usual, so that
u^{(q)}
solely depends on
\Phi
,
\kappa
and the exponent
q
, as well as on the “sampling density”
\delta>0
used for the covering
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
.

Remark
.

In principle, it would suffice for
\Phi
to be a
\mathcal{C}^{d+2}(D)
-diffeomorphism. However, in order to rely on standard theory, a
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
-BAPU contained in
\mathcal{D}(D)
is required. This is the sole reason for requiring
\Phi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(D)
in Definition
2.4
.

We can apply Theorem
2.5
with
\tilde{\kappa}
as in Definition
2.4
, to see that
\operatorname{Co}(\Phi,\mathbf{L}_{\kappa}^{p,q})
is a well-defined Banach space, for any
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
. In the following, the weight
v:\Lambda\rightarrow[1,\infty)
in the definition of the space
\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1}
will always be chosen as
v=v_{\Phi,\kappa}=(\tilde{\kappa}\cdot v_{0}^{d})\circ\Phi
, as per Definition
2.4
.

4.1.
The embedding
(\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1})^{\urcorner}\hookrightarrow Z^{\prime}(D)

To demonstrate that the coorbit spaces

\operatorname{Co}(\Phi,\mathbf{L}_{\kappa}^{p,q})
,
which are subspaces of
(\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1})^{\urcorner}
, and the decomposition spaces

\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{u}^{q})
, which are subspaces of
Z^{\prime}(D)
, coincide, we will establish an isomorphism between these spaces. The first step towards this goal is to show that

(\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1})^{\urcorner}\hookrightarrow Z^{\prime}(D)
. Once this embedding is established, it will be shown that it restricts to an embedding of the coorbit space into the decomposition space.

Remark 4.1
.

In the following, we will always assume that the weight
v
used to define the space
\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1}
is chosen according to Remark
2.6
, i.e., sufficiently large such that
v\gtrsim v_{\Phi,\kappa}
, with
v_{\Phi,\kappa}
as in Equation (
2.7
), for all
\kappa
of interest that are compatible with
\Phi
and such that
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}v(x,\Phi^{-1}(\bullet))
is moderate with respect to some submultiplicative weight.

To establish
(\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1})^{\urcorner}\hookrightarrow Z^{\prime}(D)
, we first consider the following “dual” statement:

Lemma 4.2
.

Assume that
\Phi:D\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\kappa:D\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
are compatible as per Definition
2.4
, and consider the space
\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1}
defined with respect to
\mathcal{G}(\theta,\Phi)
, with
0\neq\theta\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Let
Z(D)
be as in Definition
2.9
, with
\mathcal{O}=D
.
The map

\iota\ \ :\ \ Z(D)\to\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1},\ \ g\mapsto\overline{g}

is well-defined and continuous.
Precisely, for each compact
K\subset D
, there is

C_{1}=C_{1}(d,\theta,{{v}},K,\Phi)>0
satisfying

\|\overline{g}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1}}=\|\iota g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1}}\leq C_{1}\cdot\max_{|\alpha|\leq d+1}\|\partial^{\alpha}[\mathcal{F}^{-1}g]\|_{\mathbf{L}_{\infty}}

for all
g\in Z(D)

with
\mathop{\operatorname{supp}}(\mathcal{F}^{-1}g)\subset K
.

Likewise, for each
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and each compact set
K\subset D
,
there is
C_{2}=C_{2}(d,p,\theta,K,\Phi)>0
satisfying

|(V_{\theta,\Phi}\overline{g})(y,\omega)|\leq C_{2}\cdot\left(\max_{|\alpha|\leq d+p+1}\|\partial^{\alpha}[\mathcal{F}^{-1}g]\|_{\mathbf{L}_{\infty}}\right)\cdot(1+|y|)^{-(d+p+1)}\cdot{\mathds{1}}_{L_{K}}(\omega)\quad\text{for all}\quad(y,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times D,

for some compact set
L_{K}\subset D
(which only depends on
K,\theta,\Phi
) and
all
g\in Z(D)
with
\mathop{\operatorname{supp}}(\mathcal{F}^{-1}g)\subset K
.

Proof.

Let
K\subset D
be compact and
g\in Z(D)
with
\mathop{\operatorname{supp}}(\mathcal{F}^{-1}g)\subset K
.
We first note the elementary identity
\mathcal{F}\overline{g}=\overline{\mathcal{F}^{-1}g}
.
This identity shows that
\widehat{\overline{g}}
vanishes on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus D
, so that
g\in\mathbf{L}^{2,\mathcal{F}}(D)
.
Furthermore, we get for arbitrary
(y,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times D
, and with
g_{y,\omega}
and
g_{\omega}
as in
(
2.2
), that

(4.3)

\begin{split}(V_{\theta,\Phi}\overline{g})(y,\omega)&=\left\langle\overline{g},\,g_{y,\omega}\right\rangle_{\mathbf{L}^{2}}\\
({\scriptstyle{\text{Plancherel and }\mathcal{F}\overline{g}=\overline{\mathcal{F}^{-1}g}}})&=\left\langle\overline{\mathcal{F}^{-1}g},\,\mathbf{M}_{-y}\,g_{\omega}\right\rangle_{\mathbf{L}^{2}}\\
({\scriptstyle{\mathop{\operatorname{supp}}(\mathcal{F}^{-1}g)\subset D}})&=\int_{D}\overline{\mathcal{F}^{-1}g(\xi)}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{w(\Phi(\omega))}}\cdot\overline{\theta(\Phi(\xi)-\Phi(\omega))}\cdot e^{2\pi i\left\langle y,\xi\right\rangle}\,d\xi.\end{split}

Thus, if we define

F\colon D\times D\to\mathbb{C},\ (\xi,\omega)\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{w(\Phi(\omega))}}\cdot\overline{\theta(\Phi(\xi)-\Phi(\omega))},

then
F\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(D\times D)
and we have shown

(V_{\theta,\Phi}\overline{g})(y,\omega)=\left(\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left[\overline{\mathcal{F}^{-1}g}\cdot F(\cdot,\omega)\right]\right)(y)=:(\mathcal{F}^{-1}F_{g,\omega})(y)\quad\text{for all}\quad(y,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times D.

Note that
F_{g,\omega}\in\mathcal{D}(D)\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, since
\theta\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Now, note that if
(V_{\theta,\Phi}\overline{g})(y,\omega)\neq 0
, then
F_{g,\omega}\not\equiv 0
,
so that there is some
\xi\in\mathop{\operatorname{supp}}(\mathcal{F}^{-1}g)\subset K
satisfying
0\neq F(\xi,\omega)
and hence

\theta(\Phi(\xi)-\Phi(\omega))\neq 0
. Thus,
\Phi(\xi)-\Phi(\omega)\in\mathop{\operatorname{supp}}(\theta)=:K_{0}
, which finally implies

\Phi(\omega)\in\Phi(\xi)-K_{0}\subset\Phi(K)-K_{0}\qquad\text{ and hence }\qquad\omega\in\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(K)-K_{0})=:L_{K},

where
L_{K}\subset D
is compact and depends only on
K,\theta,\Phi
.

Next, by standard properties of the Fourier transform (see, e.g.,
[
10
, Theorem 8.22]
), we also have for
arbitrary
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
h\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
that

(4.4)

\begin{split}(1+|y|)^{k}\cdot|(\mathcal{F}^{-1}h)(y)|&\leq C_{k}\cdot\left(1+\sum_{j=1}^{d}|y_{j}|^{k}\right)\cdot|(\mathcal{F}^{-1}h)(y)|\\
&\leq C_{k}\cdot\left(\|h\|_{\mathbf{L}^{1}}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}\|\partial_{j}^{k}h\|_{\mathbf{L}^{1}}\right)\leq C_{k}(d+1)\cdot\max_{|\alpha|\leq k}\|\partial^{\alpha}h\|_{\mathbf{L}^{1}}\quad,\end{split}

for arbitrary
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Now, since
F\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(D\times D)
, the constant

C_{\theta,d,p,K}:=\max_{|\alpha|\leq d+p+1}\,\,\sup_{\omega\in L_{K},\xi\in K}|\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}F(\xi,\omega)|

is finite. Thus, Leibniz’s rule yields

|\partial^{\alpha}F_{g,\omega}(\xi)|=\left|\partial^{\alpha}_{\xi}\left(\overline{(\mathcal{F}^{-1}g)(\xi)}\cdot F(\xi,\omega)\right)\right|\lesssim C_{\theta,d,p,K}\cdot\max_{|\beta|\leq d+p+1}\|\partial^{\beta}[\mathcal{F}^{-1}g]\|_{\mathbf{L}_{\infty}}

for all
\xi\in K
,
\omega\in L_{K}
and
|\alpha|\leq d+p+1
, where th implied constant only depends on
d,p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
But by what we showed above and since
\mathop{\operatorname{supp}}(\mathcal{F}^{-1}g)\subset K
,
the left-hand side vanishes for all
(\xi,\omega)\in(D\times D)\setminus(K\times L_{K})
,
so that the estimate is indeed valid for all
\xi,\omega\in D
. Thus, we finally conclude

\displaystyle|(V_{\theta,\Phi}\overline{g})(y,\omega)|

\displaystyle=|(\mathcal{F}^{-1}F_{g,\omega})(y)|

\displaystyle({\scriptstyle{\text{by Eq. }\eqref{eq:SmoothnessYieldsFourierDecay}}})

\displaystyle\lesssim\max_{|\alpha|\leq d+p+1}\|\partial^{\alpha}F_{g,\omega}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{1}}\cdot(1+|y|)^{-(d+p+1)}

\displaystyle({\scriptstyle{\text{since }\mathop{\operatorname{supp}}(F_{g,\omega})\subset\mathop{\operatorname{supp}}(\mathcal{F}^{-1}g)\subset K}})

\displaystyle\lesssim\max_{|\alpha|\leq d+p+1}\|\partial^{\alpha}[\mathcal{F}^{-1}g]\|_{\mathbf{L}_{\infty}}\cdot\mu(K)\cdot(1+|y|)^{-(d+p+1)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\max_{|\alpha|\leq d+p+1}\|\partial^{\alpha}[\mathcal{F}^{-1}g]\|_{\mathbf{L}_{\infty}}\cdot(1+|y|)^{-(d+p+1)}.

Noting that the implied constant depends precisely un
d,p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\theta
,
\Phi
and
K\subset D
and that
(V_{\theta,\Phi}\overline{g})(y,\omega)=0
unless
\omega\in L_{K}
,
we have established the second part of the lemma.

For the first part, simply apply the preceding estimate (with
p=0
) to obtain

\displaystyle\|\overline{g}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1}}

\displaystyle=\|V_{\theta,\Phi}\overline{g}\|_{\mathbf{L}_{v}^{1}}=\int_{D}\|{v}(\omega)\cdot(V_{\theta,\Phi}\overline{g})(\cdot,\omega)\|_{\mathbf{L}^{1}}\,d\omega

\displaystyle\lesssim\|(1+|\cdot|)^{-(d+1)}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{1}}\cdot\int_{L_{K}}{v}(\omega)\,d\omega<\infty

where the last step used that
L_{K}\subset D
is compact and that
{v}
is continuous,
so that
\int_{L_{K}}{v}(\omega)\,d\omega<\infty
.
∎

Using the preceding lemma, we now get the embedding
(\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1})^{\urcorner}\hookrightarrow Z^{\prime}(D)
by duality. Some care is required, however, since the space
(\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1})^{\urcorner}
consists of
antilinear
functionals,
while
Z^{\prime}(D)
consists of linear functionals. The following corollary also takes a first step towards establishing a connection
between the voice transform of
f\in(\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1})^{\urcorner}
and an expression related to the decomposition space norm of certain functionals in
Z^{\prime}(D)
.

Corollary 4.3
.

Assume that
\Phi:D\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\kappa:D\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
are compatible and let
\iota\ \ :\ \ Z(D)\to\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1},\ \ g\mapsto\overline{g}
be as in Lemma
4.2
.
For
f\in(\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1})^{\urcorner}
, we define
\psi_{f}=f\circ\iota
. Then,

\psi_{f}:Z(D)\to\mathbb{C},g\mapsto f(\overline{g})=\langle f,\overline{g}\rangle_{(\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1})^{\urcorner},\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1}}.

is a well-defined continuous
linear
functional, i.e., an element of
Z^{\prime}(D)
. Furthermore, the
linear
map

\Psi:(\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1})^{\urcorner}\to Z^{\prime}(D),f\mapsto\psi_{f}

is continuous (with respect to the weak-
\ast
-topology on
(\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1})^{\urcorner}
), and injective.

Finally, with
g_{\omega}=[w(\Phi(\omega))]^{-1/2}\cdot(\mathbf{T}_{\Phi(\omega)}\theta)\circ\Phi
as usual,
we have

(4.5)

(V_{\theta,\Phi}f)(y,\omega)=\left(\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\overline{g_{\omega}}\cdot\mathcal{F}\psi_{f}]\right)(y)\quad\text{for all}\quad(y,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times D\text{ and }f\in(\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1})^{\urcorner}.

Remark 4.4
.

The preceding corollary shows that
\Psi:(\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1})^{\urcorner}\to Z^{\prime}(D)
is continuous
and injective. In order to fully justify the interpretation of
\Psi
as an
embedding
,
let us verify that
\psi_{f}=f
for
f\in\mathbf{L}^{2,\mathcal{F}}(D)
.

On the left-hand side,
\psi_{f}\in Z^{\prime}(D)
is a functional on

Z(D)=\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{D}(D))\subset\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and on the right-hand side,
we interpret the function
f\in\mathbf{L}^{2,\mathcal{F}}(D)\subset\mathbf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as a tempered distribution, by virtue of

\langle f,\phi\rangle_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)\phi(x)dx
, as usual.
In particular,
f
is thus a functional on
Z(D)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Taking into account all these “trivial” identifications, we get for arbitrary

g\in Z(D)\subset\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
that

\displaystyle\left\langle\psi_{f},g\right\rangle_{Z^{\prime},Z}

\displaystyle=\left\langle f,\overline{g}\right\rangle_{(\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1})^{\urcorner},\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1}}=\left\langle f,\overline{g}\right\rangle_{\mathbf{L}^{2}}=\left\langle f,g\right\rangle_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}}=\left\langle f,g\right\rangle_{Z^{\prime},Z}.

Hence,
f\in\mathbf{L}^{2,\mathcal{F}}(D)
satisfies
\psi_{f}=f
as elements of
Z^{\prime}
. Finally, note that the inclusion
\mathbf{L}^{2,\mathcal{F}}(D)\hookrightarrow Z^{\prime}(D)

is injective, since if
0=\left\langle f,g\right\rangle_{Z^{\prime},Z}
for all
g\in Z(D)
, then

0=\left\langle f,g\right\rangle_{Z^{\prime},Z}=\left\langle f,\overline{g}\right\rangle_{\mathbf{L}^{2}}=\langle\widehat{f},\widehat{\overline{g}}\rangle_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\langle\widehat{f},\overline{\mathcal{F}^{-1}g}\rangle_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(D)},

where the last step used
\widehat{f}=0
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus D
.
Since the above holds for all
g\in Z(D)
and
Z(D)=\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{D}(D))
,
we conclude that
\widehat{f}=0
almost everywhere on
D
, and thus
f=0
.
Altogether, these considerations justify the interpretation of
\Psi

as a
canonical embedding

\Psi:(\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1})^{\urcorner}\hookrightarrow Z^{\prime}(D)
.

Proof of Corollary
4.3
.

In view of Lemma
4.2
,
the first assertion follows, i.e.,
\psi_{f}\in Z^{\prime}(D)
.
Thus, we only have to prove continuity and injectivity of
\Psi
,
as well as validity of (
4.5
).

For continuity of
\Psi
, assume that the net
(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
in
(\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1})^{\urcorner}
satisfies

f_{\alpha}\to f
in the weak-
\ast
-sense. Then
f_{\alpha}(h)\to f(h)
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1}
.
Since
\overline{g}\in\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1}
for all
g\in Z(D)=\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{D}(D))
, this implies
for arbitrary
g\in Z(D)
that
\psi_{f_{\alpha}}(g)=f_{\alpha}(\overline{g})\to f(\overline{g})=\psi_{f}(g)
,
and hence
\psi_{f_{\alpha}}\to\psi_{f}
in
Z^{\prime}(D)
, since this space is equipped with the weak-
\ast
-topology.

Next, we verify Equation (
4.5
).
To this end, first note that
\theta\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
implies by definition of
g_{\omega}
(Equation (
2.2
))
that
g_{\omega}\in\mathcal{D}(D)
. Now, note

\overline{g_{y,\omega}}=\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{M}_{y}\overline{\rule{0.0pt}{7.11317pt}g_{\omega}})\in Z(D)
,
such that we obtain

\displaystyle(V_{\theta,\Phi}f)(y,\omega)

\displaystyle=\left\langle f,g_{y,\omega}\right\rangle_{(\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1})^{\urcorner},\mathcal{H}_{v}^{1}}=\psi_{f}(\overline{g_{y,\omega}})=\langle\psi_{f},\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{M}_{y}\overline{g_{\omega}})\rangle_{Z^{\prime}(D),Z(D)}=\left\langle\mathcal{F}\psi_{f},\mathbf{M}_{y}\overline{g_{\omega}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(D),\mathcal{D}(D)}\,\,.

Now, recall from the definition of the Fourier transform on
Z^{\prime}(D)
(Definition
2.9
)
that
\mathcal{F}\psi_{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(D)
and note
