w^{(N)}:=w^{(N,p)}:=(w_{\ell}^{(N,p)})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}
, with

w_{\ell}^{(N,p)}=|\det S_{\ell}|^{1/p}\cdot\Big[\inf_{\xi\in B_{d,\ell^{\ast}}}(1+|\xi|)\Big]^{-N}\quad\text{for}\quad\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\,,

satisfies
w^{(N)}\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d})\subset\ell^{q}(\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d})
.

Proof.

It is well-known that the inhomogeneous Besov covering
\mathcal{B}=(B_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}=(T_{j}B_{j}^{\prime})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}

is a semi-structured covering of
\mathbb{R}
, with
T_{j}=2^{j}
for
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
B_{0}^{\prime}=(-2,2)
and
B_{j}^{\prime}=(-2,-1/2)\cup(1/2,2)
for
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence, the elements of
\mathcal{B}_{d}
can be represented as
B_{d,\ell}=S_{\ell}B_{d,\ell}^{\prime}
, with
S_{\ell}=\mathrm{diag}(2^{\ell_{1}},\dots,2^{\ell_{d}})
and
B_{d,\ell}^{\prime}=B_{\ell_{1}}^{\prime}\times\cdots\times B_{\ell_{d}}^{\prime}
for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
. In particular,
|\det S_{\ell}|=2^{\|\ell\|_{1}}
and
\|S_{\ell}^{-1}\|\leq 1
. That
\mathcal{B}_{d}
is semi-structured now follows easily.

In Section
7
we already noted that
1+|\xi|\asymp 2^{j}
for all
\xi\in B_{j}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and it is easily seen that even
1+|\xi|\asymp 2^{j}
for all
\xi\in B_{j^{\ast}}
. Since
B_{d,\ell^{\ast}}=B_{\ell_{1}^{\ast}}\times\cdots\times B_{\ell_{d}^{\ast}}
, we obtain

1+|\xi|\asymp 1+\|\xi\|_{\infty}\asymp 2^{\|\ell\|_{\ell^{\infty}}}\quad\text{for}\quad\xi\in B_{d,\ell^{\ast}}\,.

Therefore, if
N>d/p

w_{\ell}^{(N,p)}\lesssim 2^{\|\ell\|_{1}/p}\cdot 2^{-N\|\ell\|_{\ell^{\infty}}}\lesssim 2^{(\frac{d}{p}-N)\cdot\|\ell\|_{\ell^{\infty}}}\lesssim(1+\|\ell\|_{\ell^{\infty}})^{-(d+1)}\quad\text{for}\quad\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\,,

such that
w^{(N)}\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d})
. In the last step, we used the elementary estimate

2^{x}=e^{x\cdot\ln 2}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(x\cdot\ln 2)^{k}/k!\gtrsim(1+x)^{d+1}
, valid for
x\geq 0
.
∎

Corollary 9.4
.

For
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
and
s\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and with
\mathcal{B}_{d}
and
\{{\psi_{\ell}}\}_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}
the
\mathcal{B}_{d}
-BAPU as in Definition
9.1
, then every
g\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{B}_{d},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{v^{(s)}}^{q})
extends to a tempered distribution
\tilde{g}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
given by

\tilde{g}:\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\ f\mapsto\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\langle\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\hat{g}\cdot\psi_{\ell}),f\rangle_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}}.

In particular,
\langle\tilde{g},f\rangle_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}}=\langle g,f\rangle_{Z^{\prime},Z}
, for all
f\in Z(D)
and
g\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{B}_{d},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{v^{(s)}}^{q})
.

Proof.

This is a direct application of
[
36
, Theorem 8.3]
, provided that all its assumptions are satisfied. As discussed in Remark
9.2
,
\{{\psi_{\ell}}\}_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}
is a regular partition of unity subordinate to
\mathcal{B}_{d}
, such that
\mathcal{B}_{d}
itself is a regular covering in the sense of
[
36
, Definition 8.1]
. Note that
\max\{1,\|S^{-1}_{\ell}\|\}=1
for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{d}_{0}
, as observed in the proof of Proposition
9.3
, such that the weight
w^{(N,p)}
in said proposition coincides with the weight
w^{(N)}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}
defined in
[
36
, Theorem 8.3]
. Therefore Proposition
9.3
shows the existence of
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, such that
w^{(N)}
is summable and all prerequisites of
[
36
, Theorem 8.3]
are satisfied. This completes the proof.
∎

In the above proof, and in Remark
9.2
, we did not derive the desired properties of the product weight
v^{(s)}
in full detail. Although these derivations are rather straightforward, we provide, for the convenience of the reader, a full proof in Appendix
B
, together with general results on products of semi-structured coverings. There, we also show that the inheritance of almost subordinateness, which will be used in Theorem
9.6
, is a simple consequence of the product construction.

The warping function that generates spaces of dominating mixed smoothness will turn out to be a
d
-dimensional
tensor product
, the factors of which are given by the
1
-dimensional warping function
\Phi_{\varrho_{1}}
considered in our treatment of Besov spaces in Section
7
. Therefore, the following lemma, which considers general
separable
warping functions
\Phi
, as they were referred to previously
[
19
]
, will be helpful.

Lemma 9.5
.

For
i=1,\dots,N
let
\Phi_{i}:D_{i}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
be a

k
-admissible warping function with control weight
v_{i}
.
Let
d:=d_{1}+\dots+d_{N}
and
D:=D_{1}\times\cdots\times D_{N}
.
Then

\Phi:=\Phi_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Phi_{N}\colon D=D_{1}\times\cdots\times D_{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},\ (\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{N})\mapsto\big(\Phi_{1}(\xi_{1}),\dots,\Phi_{N}(\xi_{N})\big)

is a
k
-admissible warping function with control weight

v:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+},\tau\mapsto\max\{v_{1}(|\tau|e_{1}^{(d_{1})}),\dots,v_{N}(|\tau|e_{1}^{(d_{N})})\}
, where the superscripts indicate the dimensionality of the standard unit vectors. Moreover, the
\Phi
-induced
(\delta,r)
-fine product covering

\mathcal{Q}^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi,\otimes}=\{Q^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi,\otimes,\ell}\}_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
, where
Q^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi,\otimes,\ell}=Q^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi_{1},\ell_{1}}\times\ldots\times Q^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi_{N},\ell_{N}}
, with
\ell=(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{N})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
\ell_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d_{i}}
,
i=1,\ldots,N
, is equivalent to the usual
\Phi
-induced
(\delta,r)
-fine frequency covering
\mathcal{Q}^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi}
, for all
\delta>0
,
r>\sqrt{d}/2
.

If furthermore for each
i=1,\dots,N
we are given a weight

\kappa_{i}:D_{i}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that
\kappa_{i}\circ\Phi_{i}^{-1}

is
\tilde{\kappa}_{i}
-moderate, for some continuous, radially increasing,
submultiplicative weight
\tilde{\kappa}_{i}
, then setting

\kappa:D\to\mathbb{R}^{+},(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{N})\mapsto\prod_{i=1}^{N}\kappa_{i}(\xi_{i})\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\kappa}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+},\xi\mapsto\prod_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{\kappa}_{i}(|\xi|)\,,

the weight
\kappa_{\Phi}=\kappa\circ\Phi^{-1}
is
\tilde{\kappa}
-moderate,
and
\tilde{\kappa}
is continuous, radially increasing, and submultiplicative. Hence, if
\Phi_{i}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-diffeomorphism, for all
i=1,\dots,N
, then
(\Phi,\kappa)
forms a compatible pair as per Definition
2.4
.

Proof.

Across the proof, we will identify
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with

\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{N}}
, and likewise

\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
with
\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d_{N}}
.

Since each
\Phi_{i}
is a
C^{k+1}
-diffeomorphism, so is
\Phi
.
The inverse of
\Phi
is given by

\Phi^{-1}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to D,(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{N})\mapsto\big(\Phi_{1}^{-1}(\tau_{1}),\dots,\Phi_{N}^{-1}(\tau_{N})\big)\,.

Therefore, if we set
A_{i}:=D\Phi_{i}^{-1}
, then

A(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{N}):=D\Phi^{-1}(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{N})=\mathrm{diag}\big(A_{1}(\tau_{1}),\dots,A_{N}(\tau_{N})\big)

is a block-diagonal matrix, so that

(9.3)

w(\tau)=\det(A(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{N}))=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\det(A_{i}(\tau_{i}))=\prod_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}(\tau_{i})>0\,,

where
w
and
w_{i}
are the weights associated with the warping functions
\Phi
and
\Phi_{i}
, respectively.

Furthermore, if we set

\phi_{\tau}^{(i)}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}):=A_{i}^{T}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}+\tau)\cdot A_{i}^{-T}(\tau)

for
\tau,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
, then for

\tau=(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and

{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}=({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{1},\dots,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

\phi_{\tau}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}):=A^{T}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}+\tau)\cdot A^{-T}(\tau)=\mathrm{diag}\big(\phi_{\tau_{1}}^{(1)}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{1}),\dots,\phi_{\tau_{N}}^{(N)}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{N})\big)\,.

Therefore, for any
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{N})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}

with
|\alpha|\leq k
, we have

\displaystyle\|\partial^{\alpha}\phi_{\tau}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})\|

\displaystyle=\big\|\mathrm{diag}\big(\partial^{\alpha_{1}}\phi_{\tau_{1}}^{(1)}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{1}),\dots,\partial^{\alpha_{N}}\phi_{\tau_{N}}^{(N)}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{N})\big)\big\|=\max\big\{\|\partial^{\alpha_{i}}\phi_{\tau_{i}}^{(i)}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{i})\|\,:\,i=1,\dots,N\big\}

\displaystyle\overset{(\lozenge)}{\leq}\max\big\{v_{i}(|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{i}|e_{1}^{(d_{i})})\,:\,i=1,\dots,N\big\}\overset{(\ast)}{\leq}\max\big\{v_{i}(|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}|e_{1}^{(d_{i})})\,:\,i=1,\dots,N\big\}=:v({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})\,.

Here, the step marked with
(\lozenge)
used that each
\Phi_{i}
is

k
-admissible with control weight
v_{i}
, and the step marked with

(\ast)
used that
v_{i}
is radially increasing
and that
|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{i}|\leq|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}|
.

It is clear that
v
is continuous and radially increasing.
Furthermore,

v_{i}(|\tau+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}|e_{1}^{(d_{i})})\leq v_{i}(|\tau|e_{1}^{(d_{i})}+|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}|e_{1}^{(d_{i})})\leq v_{i}(|\tau|e_{1}^{(d_{i})})\cdot v_{i}(|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}|e_{1}^{(d_{i})})\leq v(\tau)\cdot v({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})

for all
i=1,\dots,N
, which implies that
v
is submultiplicative.
In a similar manner, one can show that
\tilde{\kappa}
is continuous, radially increasing,
and submultiplicative.

Finally, to see that
\kappa_{\Phi}
is
\tilde{\kappa}
-moderate,
note for
\tau=(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and

{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}=({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{1},\dots,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that

\displaystyle(\kappa_{\Phi})(\tau+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{N}(\kappa_{i}\circ\Phi_{i}^{-1})(\tau_{i}+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{i})\leq\prod_{i=1}^{N}\left[(\kappa_{i}\circ\Phi_{i}^{-1})(\tau_{i})\cdot\tilde{\kappa}_{i}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}_{i})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{\kappa}_{i}(|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}}|)\Big)\cdot(\kappa_{\Phi})(\tau)=\tilde{\kappa}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\Upsilon}})\cdot(\kappa_{\Phi})(\tau)\,.

To finish the proof, we will now show that
\mathcal{Q}^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi,\otimes}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{Q}^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi}
. For clarity, we will indicate the dimensionality of an open ball by a superscript in the remainder of the proof, e.g.,
B_{r}^{(d)}(\xi)
denotes the
d
-dimensional open ball of radius
r>0
around
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. It is easy to see that

\Phi(Q^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi,\ell})=B_{\delta r}^{(d)}(\delta\ell)\subset B_{\delta r}^{(d_{1})}(\delta\ell_{1})\times\ldots\times B_{\delta r}^{(d_{N})}(\delta\ell_{N})=\Phi(Q^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi,\otimes,\ell}),\ \text{for all }\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},

such that
\mathcal{Q}^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi}
is subordinate to
\mathcal{Q}^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi,\otimes}
, in particular, it is almost subordinate. On the other hand,

\Phi(Q^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi,\otimes,\ell})=B_{\delta r}^{(d_{1})}(\delta\ell_{1})\times\ldots\times B_{\delta r}^{(d_{N})}(\delta\ell_{N})\subset B_{\delta\cdot\sqrt{d}r}^{(d)}(\delta\ell)=\Phi(Q^{(\delta,\sqrt{d}r)}_{\Phi,\ell}),\ \text{for all }\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},

i.e.,
\mathcal{Q}^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi,\otimes}
is subordinate to
\mathcal{Q}^{(\delta,\sqrt{d}r)}_{\Phi}
and therefore almost subordinate to
\mathcal{Q}^{(\delta,r)}_{\Phi}
, which is equivalent to
\mathcal{Q}^{(\delta,\sqrt{d}r)}_{\Phi}
by Lemma
3.2
.
∎

We are now ready to show that the spaces of dominating smoothness of Besov type
can be realized as certain warped coorbit spaces.

Theorem 9.6
.

Let
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
,
s\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\varrho_{1}
any slow start version of
the weakly
k
-admissible radial component
\varsigma_{1}=\ln(1+\bullet)
.
If
\Phi_{\varrho_{1}}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the
1
-dimensional radial warping function
associated with
\varrho_{1}
and

\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d},\ \xi\mapsto(\Phi_{\varrho_{1}}(\xi_{1}),\ldots,\Phi_{\varrho_{1}}(\xi_{d})),

then with

\kappa:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+},\xi=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{d})\mapsto\prod_{i=1}^{d}(1+|\xi_{i}|)^{s_{i}+2^{-1}-q^{-1}}\,,

we have

S^{s}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{B}_{d},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{v^{(s)}}^{q})=\operatorname{Co}(\Phi,\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa})\,,

up to canonical identifications. Here,
S^{s}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
v^{(s)}
are as in Definition
9.1
,

Proof.

Combining Remark
9.2
and Proposition
9.4
, we have already shown that
S^{s}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{B}_{d},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{v^{(s)}}^{q})
up to canonical identifications.

It remains to prove that the decomposition space
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{B}_{d},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{v^{(s)}}^{q})
coincides with the
warped coorbit space
\operatorname{Co}(\Phi,\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa})
. We have already discussed in Section
7
, that the warping function
\Phi_{\varrho_{1}}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is smooth and
d+1
-admissible and that
Q_{\Phi_{\varrho_{1}},k}^{(\delta,r)}
is equivalent to the
1
-dimensional, inhomogeneous Besov covering
\mathcal{B}
, for any
\delta>0
and
r>\sqrt{d}/2
.

Furthermore, Lemma
7.8

shows that if we set

\kappa_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+},\xi\mapsto(1+|\xi|)^{s_{i}+2^{-1}-q^{-1}}
,
then
\kappa_{i}\circ\Phi_{\varrho_{1}}
is
\tilde{\kappa}_{i}
-moderate for some
continuous, submultiplicative, radially increasing weight

\tilde{\kappa}_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Noting that
\kappa(\xi)=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\kappa_{i}(\xi_{i})
, we conclude that Lemma
9.5
shows
that
(\Phi,\kappa)
is a compatible pair, see Definition
2.4
.
Thus, Theorem
4.16
shows that

\operatorname{Co}(\Phi,\mathbf{L}_{\kappa}^{p,q})=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{u}^{q})

up to canonical identifications, with

u_{k}=\kappa_{\Phi}(\delta k)\cdot[w(\delta k)]^{q^{-1}-2^{-1}}
. Here, by Equation (
9.3
) in the proof of Lemma
9.5
, with
w_{1}=\det(\Phi^{-1}_{\varrho_{1}}(\bullet))
, the weight
w
is given by
w(\tau)=\prod_{i=1}^{d}w_{1}(\tau_{i})
.

As a direct consequence of Equation (
7.18
) in the proof of Theorem
7.10
, we can use Proposition
6.8
and Lemma
7.3
to obtain weak equivalence of
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi_{\varrho_{1}}}^{(\delta,r)}
and by extension of
\mathcal{B}_{d}
and the product covering
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi,\otimes}^{(\delta,r)}
defined in Lemma
9.5
. By that lemma,
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi,\otimes}^{(\delta,r)}
is further equivalent to the usual
\Phi
-induced
(\delta,r)
-fine frequency covering
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
, such that in total,
\mathcal{B}_{d}
is weakly equivalent to
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
.

Hence, Theorem
2.13
shows that

\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{u}^{q})=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{B}_{d},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{v^{(s)}}^{q})
, provided that

(9.4)

u_{k}\asymp v_{\ell}^{(s)}\quad\text{for all}\quad\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\text{ and }k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\text{ satisfying }Q_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}\cap B_{d,\ell}\neq\varnothing\,.

Using
Q_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}\subset Q_{\Phi,\otimes,k}^{(\delta,r)}
, we can instead show that (
9.4
) holds for
\ell,k
such that
Q_{\Phi,\otimes,k}^{(\delta,r)}\cap B_{d,\ell}\neq\varnothing
. Since
\xi\in Q_{\Phi,\otimes,k}^{(\delta,r)}\cap B_{d,\ell}
is equivalent to
\xi_{i}\in Q_{\Phi_{\varrho_{1}},k_{i}}\cap B_{\ell_{i}}
and both
u
and
v^{(s)}
have product structure, we can verify
u_{k}\asymp v_{\ell}^{(s)}
componentwise. However,

(1+|\xi_{i}|)^{s_{i}+2^{-1}-q^{-1}}\cdot[w(\delta k)]^{q^{-1}-2^{-1}}\asymp(1+|\xi_{i}|)^{s_{i}}\asymp 2^{s_{i}\ell_{i}}

is a direct consequence of Equations (
6.10
) and (
7.5
), after noting that
1+(\varrho_{1})_{\ast}(|\tau|)=(\varrho_{1})_{\ast}^{\prime}(|\tau|)=e^{|\tau|}
. Hence, we can apply Theorem
2.13
to obtain

\operatorname{Co}(\Phi,\mathbf{L}_{\kappa}^{p,q})=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{u}^{q})=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{B}_{d},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{v^{(s)}}^{q})=S^{s}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

as desired.
∎

Remark 9.7
.

Using Lemma
9.5
and the equality
\operatorname{Co}(\Phi,\mathbf{L}_{\kappa}^{p,q})=S^{s}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, it is straightforward to extend
Definition
9.1
to obtain a larger family of anisotropic
smoothness spaces. In particular, choosing
\alpha\in(-\infty,1]^{d}
and with
\varsigma_{{\alpha_{i}}}=(1+\bullet)^{(1-\alpha_{i})}
if
\alpha_{i}\in(-\infty,1)
, and
\varsigma_{1}=\ln(1+\bullet)
otherwise, we can define
anisotropic
\alpha
-modulation spaces
as
\operatorname{Co}(\Phi_{\alpha},\mathbf{L}_{\kappa}^{p,q})
with an appropriate choice of
\kappa
and
\Phi_{\varrho_{\alpha}}=\Phi_{\varrho_{{\alpha_{1}}}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\Phi_{\varrho_{{\alpha_{d}}}}
, where each
\varrho_{{\alpha_{i}}}
is a slow start version of
\varsigma_{{\alpha_{i}}}
, and the radial warping function
\Phi_{\varrho_{{\alpha_{i}}}}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined as usual.

A rather straightforward adaptation of Proposition
8.5
using the considerations in the proof of Theorem
9.6
yields embeddings between these
anisotropic
\alpha
-modulation spaces
and spaces of dominating mixed smoothness.

10.
Conclusion

In this paper we have shown that warped coorbit spaces
\operatorname{Co}(\Phi,\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa})
can be identified with certain decomposition spaces. This has allowed us to derive embedding relations between warped coorbit spaces and study the relations between these spaces and semi-classical smoothness spaces. In particular, we have shown that certain warped coorbit spaces coincide with
\alpha
-modulation spaces and (Besov-type) spaces of dominating mixed smoothness. For Besov spaces spaces, we have shown that such equality is only possible when considering functions on the real line. For Besov spaces of functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
d>1
, we have proposed a warping function that achieves a certain
closeness
relation between the associated warped coorbit spaces and Besov spaces.

Appendix A
\alpha
-coverings for general
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}

In this appendix, we collect two secondary facts about
\alpha
-coverings.
As our first result, we show that any two
\alpha
-coverings, with respect to the same
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
are weakly equivalent.
This generalizes
[
2
, Lemma B.2]
, where
the same observation is made for
\alpha\in[0,1]
.
As our second result, we show that there are no
\alpha
-coverings for

\alpha>1
, such that
\alpha\in(-\infty,1]
, as considered in Section
8
, is
indeed the most general setting. For ease of reference, we recall the definition of an
\alpha
-covering, where, for now, we only assume
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
.

Definition A.1
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}=(Q_{i})_{i\in I}
be an admissible covering of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
and let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
.
The family
\mathcal{Q}
is called an
\alpha
-covering of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, if the
following hold:

(1)

With

(A.1)

\quad\quad\quad r_{Q}:=\sup\{r>0\,:\,\exists\,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:B_{r}(\xi)\subset Q\}\quad\!\!\text{and}\quad\!\!R_{Q}:=\inf\{R>0\,:\,\exists\,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:Q\subset B_{R}(\xi)\}\,,

we have

\sup_{i\in I}R_{Q_{i}}/r_{Q_{i}}<\infty
.

(2)

We have
\mu(Q_{i})\asymp(1+|\xi|)^{\alpha\cdot d}

for all
i\in I
and
\xi\in Q_{i}
,
where the implied constant does not depend on
i,\xi
.

Below, we will repeatedly use the following consequence of the definition of

\alpha
-coverings: If
\mathcal{Q}=(Q_{i})_{i\in I}
is an
\alpha
-covering,
then
r_{Q_{i}}^{d}\lesssim\mu(Q_{i})\lesssim R_{Q_{i}}^{d}\lesssim r_{Q_{i}}^{d}
,
and hence

(A.2)

(1+|\xi|)^{\alpha d}\asymp\mu(Q_{i})\asymp R_{Q_{i}}^{d}\asymp r_{Q_{i}}^{d}\qquad\forall\,i\in I\text{ and }\xi\in Q_{i}\,.

The next result shows that the specific
\alpha
-covering chosen to define
M^{s,\alpha}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is inconsequential, by Theorem
2.13
.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, and let
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{P}
be two
\alpha
-coverings
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{P}
are weakly equivalent.

Proof.

Before we properly start the proof, we observe for any bounded set

Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
that
|\xi-\eta|\leq 2R_{Q}
for all
\xi,\eta\in Q
.
Indeed, for arbitrary
R>R_{Q}
, there is some

\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
Q\subset\zeta+B_{R}(0)
, and hence

|\xi-\eta|\leq|\xi-\zeta|+|\zeta-\eta|\leq 2R
.

Now, write
\mathcal{Q}=(Q_{i})_{i\in I}
and
\mathcal{P}=(P_{j})_{j\in J}
.
By symmetry, it suffices to show that there is some
N\in\mathbb{N}
with

|I_{j}|\leq N
for all
j\in J
, with

I_{j}=\{i\in I\,:\,Q_{i}\cap P_{j}\neq\varnothing\}
.

To see this, fix
j\in J
and
\eta\in P_{j}
.
For any
i\in I_{j}
, there is some
\xi_{i}\in Q_{i}\cap P_{j}
.
For arbitrary
\xi\in Q_{i}
, this implies

\displaystyle|\xi-\eta|

\displaystyle\leq|\xi-\xi_{i}|+|\xi_{i}-\eta|\leq 2(R_{Q_{i}}+R_{P_{j}})

\displaystyle({\scriptstyle{\text{Eq.~}\eqref{eq:AlphaCoveringAsymptotic}\text{ and }\xi_{i}\in Q_{i}\cap P_{j}}})

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+|\xi_{i}|)^{\alpha}

\displaystyle({\scriptstyle{\xi_{i}\in P_{j}\text{ and }\eta\in P_{j}}})

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+|\eta|)^{\alpha}\,.

Therefore,
Q_{i}\subset B_{C(1+|\eta|)^{\alpha}}(\eta)
for a constant

C=C(d,\alpha,\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{P})
independent of
i,j
.
Since we also have
\sum_{i\in I_{j}}{\mathds{1}}_{Q_{i}}\lesssim 1
by the
admissibility of
\mathcal{Q}
, we see
\sum_{i\in I_{j}}{\mathds{1}}_{Q_{i}}\lesssim{\mathds{1}}_{B_{C(1+|\eta|)^{\alpha}}(\eta)}
, and hence

\displaystyle(1+|\eta|)^{\alpha d}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\mu(B_{C(1+|\eta|)^{\alpha}}(\eta))\gtrsim\int\sum_{i\in I_{j}}{\mathds{1}}_{Q_{i}}\,dx=\sum_{i\in I_{j}}\mu(Q_{i})

\displaystyle({\scriptstyle{\text{Eq. }\eqref{eq:AlphaCoveringAsymptotic}}})

\displaystyle\gtrsim\sum_{i\in I_{j}}(1+|\xi_{i}|)^{\alpha d}

\displaystyle({\scriptstyle{\text{Eq. }\eqref{eq:AlphaCoveringAsymptotic}\text{ and }\xi_{i},\eta\in P_{j}}})

\displaystyle\gtrsim\sum_{i\in I_{j}}(1+|\eta|)^{\alpha d}=|I_{j}|\cdot(1+|\eta|)^{\alpha d}\,,

which implies
|I_{j}|\lesssim 1
, as desired.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

Let
\alpha>1
and
d\in\mathbb{N}
be arbitrary.
Then there does not exist an
\alpha
-covering of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proof.

Assume towards a contradiction that
\mathcal{Q}=(Q_{i})_{i\in I}
is such a
covering. As seen in Equation (
A.2
), there is
a constant
c>0
with
r_{Q_{i}}\geq 2c\cdot(1+|\eta|)^{\alpha}

for all
i\in I
and
\eta\in Q_{i}
.

By definition of
r_{Q}
, there is for each
i\in I
some
\xi_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

such that

\xi_{i}+B_{c\,(1+|\eta|)^{\alpha}}(0)\subset\xi_{i}+B_{r_{Q_{i}}/2}\,(0)\subset Q_{i}\qquad\forall\,i\in I\text{ and }\eta\in Q_{i}\,.

Next, since
\mathcal{Q}
covers all of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, there is for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
some

i_{n}\in I
satisfying
\eta_{n}:=(n,0,\dots,0)\in Q_{i_{n}}
.
Therefore,

\xi_{i_{n}}+B_{c\,\cdot n^{\alpha}}(0)\subset\xi_{i_{n}}+B_{c\,(1+|\eta_{n}|)^{\alpha}}\,(0)\subset Q_{i_{n}}\,.

It is not hard to see that there is some

\zeta_{n}\in B_{c\cdot n^{\alpha}}(0)
satisfying
|\xi_{i_{n}}+\zeta_{n}|=|\xi_{i_{n}}|+\frac{c}{2}\cdot n^{\alpha}\gtrsim n^{\alpha}
.
However, since
\xi_{i_{n}}+\zeta_{n}\in Q_{i_{n}}
,
\eta_{n}\in Q_{i_{n}}
,
and
\alpha d\neq 0
, Equation (
A.2
)
implies

n\asymp 1+|\eta_{n}|\asymp 1+|\xi_{i_{n}}+\zeta_{n}|\gtrsim n^{\alpha}\,.

Since this holds for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, with the implicit constant being
independent of
n
, and since
\alpha>1
, this yields the desired
contradiction.
∎

Appendix B
Products of semi-structured coverings

For the sake of completeness, we prove that the product of several
(tight) semi-structured coverings is again (tight and) semi-structured, that subordinateness relations are inherited by the product covering and moderateness relations between coverings and weights are inherited by the product covering and a separable product of the weights.

Lemma B.1
.

For each
\ell=1,\dots,N
, let

\mathcal{Q}_{\ell}=(Q_{i}^{(\ell)})_{i\in I_{\ell}}=(T_{i}^{(\ell)}P_{i}^{(\ell)}+b_{i}^{(\ell)})_{i\in I_{\ell}}

be a semi-structured covering of an open set
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d_{\ell}}
.
Let
I:=I_{1}\times\cdots\times I_{N}
and

d:=d_{1}+\dots+d_{N}
, and for
i=(i_{1},\dots,i_{N})\in I
set

T_{i}:=\mathrm{diag}\big(T_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\dots,T_{i_{N}}^{(N)}\big)\in\mathrm{GL}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,,\quad b_{i}:=\big(b_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\dots,b_{i_{N}}^{(N)}\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,,\quad Q_{i}^{\prime}:=P_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\times\cdots\times P_{i_{N}}^{(N)}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\,.

Then the following hold:

(1)

The covering

\mathcal{Q}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{Q}_{N}:=\big(Q_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\times\cdots\times Q_{i_{N}}^{(N)}\big)_{i\in I}=\big(T_{i}\,Q_{i}^{\prime}+b_{i}\big)_{i\in I}

is a semi-structured covering of

\mathcal{O}:=\mathcal{O}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{O}_{N}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If the
\mathcal{Q}_{\ell}
,
\ell=1,\dots,N
,
are tight, then so is
\mathcal{Q}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{Q}_{N}
.

(2)

If
v^{(\ell)}
is a
\mathcal{Q}_{\ell}
-moderate
weight, for
\ell=1,\dots,N
, then the weight
v=(v_{i})_{i\in I}
, with

v_{i}=\prod_{\ell=1}^{N}v_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}
is

\mathcal{Q}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{Q}_{N}
-moderate.

(3)

If
\mathcal{Q}_{\ell}=(Q_{i}^{(\ell)})_{i\in I_{\ell}}

and
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}=(P_{j}^{(\ell)})_{j\in J_{\ell}}
, for
\ell=1,\dots,N
, are admissible coverings of

\mathcal{O}_{\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d_{\ell}}
, and if, for all
\ell
,
\mathcal{Q}_{\ell}
is weakly subordinate to

\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
, then
\mathcal{Q}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{Q}_{N}
is
weakly subordinate to
\mathcal{P}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{P}_{N}
. If the
\mathcal{Q}_{\ell}
are even almost subordinate to
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
, then
\mathcal{Q}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{Q}_{N}
is
almost subordinate to
\mathcal{P}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{P}_{N}
.

Proof.

For brevity, let
\mathcal{Q}:=\mathcal{Q}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{Q}_{N}
.
Clearly,
\mathcal{Q}
is indeed a covering of
\mathcal{O}
.
To prove the semi-structuredness of
\mathcal{Q}
, first note that for each

\ell=1,\dots,N
, there is some
R_{\ell}>0
such that

Q_{i}^{(\ell)}\subset\overline{B_{R_{\ell}}(0)}
for all
i\in I_{\ell}
.
If we set
R:=\sqrt{N}\cdot\max\{R_{\ell}\,:\,\ell=1,\dots,N\}
,
then this implies
Q_{i}^{\prime}\subset\overline{B_{R}(0)}
for all
i\in I
.

Next, note for
i=(i_{1},\dots,i_{N})\in I
and

j=(j_{1},\dots,j_{N})\in I
that

i\in j^{\ast}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad Q_{i}\cap Q_{j}\neq\varnothing\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\forall\,\ell=1,\dots,N\,:Q_{i_{\ell}}\cap Q_{j_{\ell}}\neq\varnothing\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad i\in\prod_{\ell=1}^{N}j_{\ell}^{\ast}\,.

On the one hand, this shows

M_{\mathcal{Q}}=\sup_{i\in I}|i^{\ast}|=\sup_{i\in I}\prod_{\ell=1}^{N}|i_{\ell}^{\ast}|\leq\prod_{\ell=1}^{N}M_{\mathcal{Q}_{\ell}}<\infty
,
so that
\mathcal{Q}
is admissible.
On the other hand, we see that if
i\in j^{\ast}
, then

\displaystyle\|T_{i}^{-1}T_{j}\|

\displaystyle=\Big\|\mathrm{diag}\big((T_{i_{1}}^{(1)})^{-1}\,T_{j_{1}}^{(1)},\dots,(T_{i_{N}}^{(N)})^{-1}\,T_{j_{N}}^{(N)}\big)\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,n\in I_{\ell}\text{ and }\\
Q_{m}^{(\ell)}\cap Q_{n}^{(\ell)}\neq\varnothing\end{subarray}}\|(T_{m}^{(\ell)})^{-1}T_{n}^{(\ell)}\|\,:\,\ell=1,\dots,N\right\}\leq\max\{C_{\mathcal{Q}_{\ell}}\,:\,\ell=1,\dots,N\}\,,

where
C_{\mathcal{Q}_{\ell}}
is the constant
C>0
in Definition
2.7
(2), for
\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{Q}_{\ell}
. Hence,
\mathcal{Q}
is a semi-structured covering of
\mathcal{O}
.

We further see that
v
is
\mathcal{Q}
-moderate,
since if
Q_{i}\cap Q_{j}\neq\varnothing
, then

\frac{v_{i}}{v_{j}}=\prod_{\ell=1}^{N}\frac{v_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}}{v_{j_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}}\leq\prod_{\ell=1}^{N}C_{v^{(\ell)},\mathcal{Q}_{\ell}}<\infty\,.

If additionally, all the coverings
\mathcal{Q}_{\ell}
are tight, then there is for each

\ell=1,\dots,N
some
\varepsilon_{\ell}>0
and for each
i\in I_{\ell}
some

c_{i}^{(\ell)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\ell}}
satisfying
B_{\varepsilon_{\ell}}(c_{i}^{(\ell)})\subset Q_{i}^{(\ell)}
.
Set
c_{i}:=(c_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\dots,c_{i_{N}}^{(N)})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

for
i=(i_{1},\dots,i_{N})\in I
and furthermore

\varepsilon:=\min\{\varepsilon_{\ell}\,:\,\ell=1,\dots,N\}
. It follows that

B_{\varepsilon}(c_{i})\subset Q_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i\in I
, i.e.,
\mathcal{Q}
is tight.

Finally, we prove the last part of the lemma. If we define

J_{i}^{(\ell)}:=\{j\in J_{\ell}\,:\,P_{j}^{(\ell)}\cap Q_{i}^{(\ell)}\neq\varnothing\}

for
i\in I_{\ell}
, then there is some
M\in\mathbb{N}
such that

|J_{i}^{(\ell)}|\leq M
for all
i\in I_{\ell}
and
\ell=1,\dots,N
,
since
\mathcal{Q}_{\ell}
is weakly subordinate to
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
.

Now, set
I:=I_{1}\times\cdots\times I_{N}
and

J:=J_{1}\times\cdots\times J_{N}
.
Furthermore, let
Q_{i}:=Q_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\times\cdots\times Q_{i_{N}}^{(N)}

and
P_{j}:=P_{j_{1}}^{(1)}\times\cdots\times P_{j_{N}}^{(N)}
for

i=(i_{1},\dots,i_{N})\in I
and
j=(j_{1},\dots,j_{N})\in J
.
Then

\begin{split}J_{i}:=\{j\in J\,:\,P_{j}\cap Q_{i}\neq\varnothing\}&=\big\{j=(j_{1},\dots,j_{N})\in J\,:\,\text{for all }\ell=1,\dots,N\,:\,P_{j_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}\cap Q_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}\neq\varnothing\big\}\\
&=J_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\times\cdots\times J_{i_{N}}^{(N)}\,,\end{split}

and hence
|J_{i}|=|J_{i_{1}}^{(1)}|\cdots|J_{i_{N}}^{(N)}|\leq M^{N}

for arbitrary
i=(i_{1},\dots,i_{N})\in I
.
Hence,
\mathcal{Q}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{Q}_{N}

is weakly subordinate to
\mathcal{P}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{P}_{N}
.

If
\mathcal{Q}_{\ell}
is almost subordinate to
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
,
\ell=1,\dots,N
, i.e., there is
M_{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
i_{\ell}\in I_{\ell}
there is a
j_{\ell}=j_{i,\ell}\in J_{\ell}
,
such that
Q^{(\ell)}_{i}\subset(P^{(\ell)}_{j})^{N_{\ell}\ast}
, then with
M=\max_{\ell}M_{\ell}
,
Q^{(\ell)}_{(i_{1},\cdot,i_{N})}\subset(P^{(\ell)}_{(j_{1},\ldots,j_{N})})^{M\ast}
follows.
∎

Appendix C
The structured
\Phi
-induced
(\delta,r)
-fine frequency covering

In this final appendix, we demonstrate that every warping function
\Phi
induces
a
structured

(\delta,r)
-fine frequency covering, for
r\in(0,\vartheta_{0})
and
\delta\in(0,\Delta_{r})
, with
\vartheta_{0}>0
as in Lemma
3.4
and a certain
\Delta_{r}>0
.

Definition C.1
.

A semi-structured covering
\mathcal{Q}=(Q_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\mathcal{O}
, with

Q_{i}=T_{i}Q_{i}^{\prime}+b_{i}
,
i\in I
, as in Definition
2.7
(2)
is called
structured
, if the operators
T_{i}
can be chosen
such that
Q_{i}^{\prime}=Q
, for all
i\in I
, and there is an open set

P\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\overline{P}\subset Q
and

\mathcal{O}=\bigcup_{i\in I}(T_{i}P+b_{i}).

It should be noted that every structured admissible covering
\mathcal{Q}
is a decomposition covering,
that is, there is a
\mathcal{Q}
-BAPU, see
[
3
, Proposition 1]
. While the covering
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
is in general not structured, we can define an equivalent family of structured coverings using Lemma
3.4
. Thus, for the purpose of investigating the associated decomposition spaces,
it does not matter which of the two families of coverings is considered, see Theorem
2.13
. However, the structured covering may simplify the derivation of certain results in future work.

Definition C.2
.

Let
\Phi:D\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a diffeomorphism.
For
\delta,r>0
, we define

\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}:=\left(S_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}:=\Big(\Phi^{-1}(\delta k)+D\Phi^{-1}(\delta k)\langle B_{r}(0)\rangle\Big)_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\,.

The family
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
is called the

structured
(\delta,r)
-fine frequency covering induced by
\Phi
.

We proceed to show that

\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\varepsilon,\rho)}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}

are equivalent, for suitable choices of the parameters.

Lemma C.3
.

Let
\Phi:D\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a
1
-admissible warping function
with control weight
v_{0}
and set
\vartheta_{0}:=(2dv_{0}(1))^{-1}
.
If
r\in(0,\vartheta_{0}/4)
and
\delta\in\big(0,\min\{1,4r\}(2\sqrt{d}\cdot v_{0}(1/2))^{-1}\big)
,
then
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
is a structured admissible covering of
D
.

Moreover,
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
is equivalent to

\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{(\delta^{\prime},r^{\prime})}
for any
r^{\prime}\in(0,\vartheta_{0}/4)
and
\delta^{\prime}\in\big(0,\min\{1,4r^{\prime}\}(2\sqrt{d}\cdot v_{0}(1/2))^{-1}\big)
, and to the coverings

\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\varepsilon,\rho)}
, for any
\varepsilon>0
,
\rho>\sqrt{d}/2
.

Proof.

Let
r\in(0,\vartheta_{0}/4)
be arbitrary, such that
\vartheta:=4r\in(0,\vartheta_{0})
and note that

\vartheta_{0}
is as in Lemma
3.4
. Hence,
Equation (
3.3
) of said lemma, with
\xi=\Phi^{-1}(\delta k)
,
yields

(C.1)

\begin{split}S_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}&=\xi+D\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(\xi))\langle B_{\vartheta/4}(0)\rangle\subset\Phi^{-1}\big(\Phi(\xi)+B_{\vartheta}(0)\big)\\
&=\Phi^{-1}\big(\delta k+B_{4r}(0)\big)=\Phi^{-1}\big(\delta\cdot B_{4r/\delta}(k)\big)\\
&\subset\Phi^{-1}\big(\delta\cdot B_{\rho^{\ast}}(k)\big)=Q_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,\rho^{\ast})}\subset D\,,\qquad\text{ whenever }\qquad\rho^{\ast}\geq 4r/\delta.\end{split}

In addition to showing that the family
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}

is subordinate (and thus almost subordinate) to the covering

\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,\rho^{\ast})}
with arbitrary

\rho^{\ast}>\max\{\frac{\sqrt{d}}{2},\frac{4r}{\delta}\}
,
this also implies for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
that

S_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}\subset D
.
Furthermore, Equation (
C.1
)
also shows that
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
is admissible, since

\left|\left\{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\,:\,S_{\Phi,\ell}^{(\delta,r)}\cap S_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}\neq\varnothing\right\}\right|\leq\left|\left\{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\,:\,Q_{\Phi,\ell}^{(\delta,\rho^{\ast})}\cap Q_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,\rho^{\ast})}\neq\varnothing\right\}\right|\leq N_{\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,\rho^{\ast})}}<\infty,

for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, by admissibility of
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,\rho^{\ast})}
(see Lemma
3.2
).

If
\rho>\sqrt{d}/2
is arbitrary,
then, by performing the same derivations as in the proof of
Lemma
3.2
, we can show that

(C.2)

Q_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,\rho)}=\Phi^{-1}(\delta\cdot B_{\rho}(k))\subset\Phi^{-1}(\delta k)+A(\delta k)\langle B_{\delta\rho\cdot v_{0}(\delta\rho)}(0)\rangle=S_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,\delta\rho\cdot v_{0}(\delta\rho))}\,.

Now, for fixed
r\in(0,\vartheta_{0}/4)
and

0<\delta<\frac{2}{\sqrt{d}}\frac{\min\{1,4r\}}{4\cdot v_{0}(1/2)}

as per our assumption, we can choose
\rho_{0}>\sqrt{d}/2
, such that

\delta\rho_{0}<\frac{\min\{1,4r\}}{4\cdot v_{0}(1/2)}\leq 1/4.

Hence, using that
v_{0}
is radially increasing, we can estimate

\delta\rho_{0}
by either
v_{0}(1/2)^{-1}r
or simply
1/4
,
such that
\delta\rho_{0}\cdot v_{0}(2\delta\rho_{0})<\frac{rv_{0}(1/2)}{v_{0}(1/2)}\leq r
.
Therefore, Equation (
C.2
) can be applied with

\rho=\rho_{0}
, yielding

Q_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,\rho_{0})}\subset S_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,\delta\rho_{0}\cdot v_{0}(2\delta\rho_{0}))}\subset S_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}\overset{\text{Equation ~}\eqref{eq:StructuredFrequencyCoveringSubordinateToFrequencyCovering}}{\subset}D\qquad\forall\,k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\,.

On the one hand, this proves that
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,\rho_{0})}
is
subordinate to
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
.
On the other hand, since
\rho_{0}>\sqrt{d}/2
,
Lemma
3.1

shows
D=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}Q_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,\rho_{0})}\subset\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}S_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}\subset D
, so that

\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
covers all of
D
.

Moreover, since all coverings
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\varepsilon,\rho)}
with

\rho>\sqrt{d}/2
and
\varepsilon>0
are equivalent,
since
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,\rho_{0})}
is subordinate to

\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
, and since we saw above that

\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
is subordinate to
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,\rho^{\ast})}

for
\rho^{\ast}>\max\{\frac{\sqrt{d}}{2},\frac{4r}{\delta}\}
,
we see that the coverings
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
and

\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\varepsilon,\rho)}
are equivalent, as long as

\rho>\sqrt{d}/2
,
r\in(0,\vartheta_{0}/4)
, and

0<\delta<\frac{\min\{1,4r\}}{2\sqrt{d}\cdot v_{0}(1/2)}
.

It remains to show that
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
is indeed
a structured covering.
But setting
T_{k}=A(\delta k)
and
b_{k}=\Phi^{-1}(\delta k)
, as well as

Q:=B_{r}(0)
, we clearly have
S_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}=T_{k}Q+b_{k}

for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
Finally, if we fix
\rho^{\ast}>\max\{\frac{\sqrt{d}}{2},\frac{4r}{\delta}\}
,
and if
S_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}\cap S_{\Phi,\ell}^{(\delta,r)}\neq\varnothing
,
then Equation (
C.1
)
shows
\varnothing\neq Q_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,\rho^{\ast})}\cap Q_{\Phi,\ell}^{(\delta,\rho^{\ast})}
,
and hence
\|T_{k}^{-1}T_{\ell}\|\leq C
for an absolute constant

C=C(\Phi,\delta,r)>0
, see Lemma
3.2
.

Finally, if we choose

\tilde{r}\in(0,r)\subset(0,\vartheta_{0}/4)
such that we still have

\delta<\frac{\min\{1,4\tilde{r}\}}{2\sqrt{d}\cdot v_{0}(1/2)}
,
then it is clear that
P:=B_{\tilde{r}}(0)
satisfies
\overline{P}\subset Q
,
and the preceding part of the proof shows that

(T_{k}P+b_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
still covers
D
.
∎

Using the equivalence between the coverings
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,\varrho)}

and
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
, we can now show that the associated
decomposition spaces also coincide.

Corollary C.4
.

With notation as in Lemma
C.3
,
let
r\in(0,\vartheta_{0}/4)
,
s>\sqrt{d}/2
, and

0<\delta<\frac{\min\{1,4r\}}{2\sqrt{d}\cdot v_{0}(1/2)}
.

If
u=(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,s)}
-moderate,
then
u
is also
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
-moderate, and we have

\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,s)},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{u}^{q})=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{u}^{q})\qquad\forall\,p,q\in[1,\infty]\,.

Proof.

Directly from the definition of the coverings
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,s)}

and
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
, we see

\Phi^{-1}(\delta k)\in S_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}\cap Q_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,s)}\neq\varnothing\qquad\forall\,k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\,.

Note that this derivation crucially uses that the
sampling density

\delta>0
of both coverings coincides.

In Lemma
C.3
, we saw that
the coverings
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,s)}
and

\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,s)}
are equivalent.
In particular, there is a fixed
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,
there is some
j_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
satisfying
S_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}\subset Q_{\Phi,j_{k}^{N\ast}}^{(\delta,s)}
.
But then

\Phi^{-1}(\delta k)\in S_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}\cap Q_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,s)}\subset Q_{\Phi,j_{k}^{N\ast}}^{(\delta,s)}\cap Q_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,s)}\,,

so that
k\in j_{k}^{(N+1)\ast}
, and hence
j_{k}\in k^{(N+1)\ast}
.
But this implies
j_{k}^{N\ast}\subset k^{(2N+1)\ast}
, and hence

(C.3)

S_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}\subset Q_{\Phi,j_{k}^{N\ast}}^{(\delta,s)}\subset Q_{\Phi,k^{(2N+1)\ast}}^{(\delta,s)}\qquad\forall\,k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\,.

The inclusion
(
C.3
) yields the

\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
-moderateness of
u
, since if

\varnothing\neq S_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,r)}\cap S_{\Phi,\ell}^{(\delta,r)}
,
then
\varnothing\neq Q_{\Phi,k^{(2N+1)\ast}}^{(\delta,s)}\cap Q_{\Phi,\ell^{(2N+1)\ast}}^{(\delta,s)}
,
and thus
\ell\in k^{(4N+3)\ast}
, so that
u_{\ell}\asymp u_{k}
. Here, we used
that
v_{i}\leq C^{\ell}\cdot v_{j}
for
j\in i^{\ell\ast}
, whenever
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
v=(v_{i})_{i\in I}
is
\mathcal{Q}
-moderate, i.e.,

v_{i}\leq C\cdot v_{j}
if
Q_{i}\cap Q_{j}\neq\varnothing
. This inequality can be verified
by a straightforward induction.

Likewise, the inclusion
(
C.3
) also implies that

u_{k}\asymp u_{\ell}
if
\varnothing\neq Q_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,s)}\cap S_{\Phi,\ell}^{(\delta,r)}
, since
then
\varnothing\neq Q_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,s)}\cap Q_{\Phi,\ell^{(2N+1)\ast}}^{(\delta,s)}
,
which implies
k\in\ell^{(2N+2)\ast}
, and hence

u_{k}\asymp u_{\ell}
.

Overall, since the coverings
\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,s)}
and

\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)}
are equivalent, and since
u_{k}\asymp u_{\ell}

if
Q_{\Phi,k}^{(\delta,s)}\cap S_{\Phi,\ell}^{(\delta,r)}\neq\varnothing
,
Theorem
2.13
shows for arbitrary

p,q\in[1,\infty]
that

\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,s)},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{u}^{q})=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{(\delta,r)},\mathbf{L}^{p},\ell_{u}^{q})
, with
equivalent norms.
∎

\markleft
